AI Podcast › 安库尔·莫伊特拉 › 本期

数学中的 AI 危机：Navier-Stokes、黎曼猜想与复杂性理论——Ankur Moitra、Steven Brunton 与 Alex Kontorovich 共谈数学的未来

AI Crisis in Mathematics: Navier-Stokes, Riemann Hypothesis, and Complexity Theory — Ankur Moitra, Steven Brunton, and Alex Kontorovich on the Future of Math

安库尔·莫伊特拉 Ankur Moitra · Lex Fridman 播客 · 2026-10-26 · 约 214 分钟 · 原视频 ↗

打开互动全文版（中英对照 + 朗读 + 问答）→

本期速览 · Overview

Ankur Moitra、Steven Brunton 与 Alex 做客 Lex Fridman 播客，共同解读机器宣称攻克 Navier-Stokes、黎曼猜想与唯一游戏猜想这一历史性时刻。

Ankur Moitra, Steven Brunton, and Alex join Lex Fridman to make sense of the moment machines claimed to solve Navier-Stokes, the Riemann Hypothesis, and the Unique Games Conjecture.

要点 · TL;DR

  • AI 数学突破不是危机，而是开启数学知识的黄金时代。
    AI math breakthroughs are not a crisis but open a golden age of mathematical knowledge.
  • 人类理解与故事仍是核心；定理证明自动化让提问更有价值。
    Human understanding and stories remain central; theorem-proving automation makes asking questions more valuable.
  • Lean 和 MathLib 实现形式验证，但理解仍需人类叙事。
    Lean and MathLib enable formal verification, but understanding still requires human narratives.

核心观点 · Key points

  • AI近期的数学突破令人震惊但并非危机，而是开启了数学知识的黄金时代。
    AI's recent math breakthroughs are shocking but not a crisis; they open a golden age of mathematical knowledge.
  • 纯数学的价值在于人类的理解和故事，而不仅仅是数据中心里的定理陈述。
    The value of pure math lies in human understanding and stories, not just theorem statements in data centers.
  • 随着定理证明自动化，提出正确问题和数学洞察力变得更有价值。
    Asking the right questions and mathematical insight become more valuable as theorem-proving gets automated.
  • Lean和MathLib实现形式化验证，使AI生成的证明可检查至公理。
    Lean and MathLib enable formal verification, making AI-generated proofs checkable down to axioms.
  • 门童谬误警告不要将数学家的工作简化为仅仅证明定理。
    The doorman fallacy warns against reducing a mathematician's job to just proving theorems.
  • 机构和指导必须适应变化；旧的成功蓝图已经过时。
    Institutions and mentorship must adapt; the old blueprint for success is obsolete.

反共识 · Contrarian takes

  • AI的解决方案不仅仅是凸包组合；它们形成一个半群，扩展了可达知识。
    AI's solutions are not just convex hull combinations; they form a semigroup that expands reachable knowledge.
  • 知道问题已解决会激励更快地重新发现，而非绝望；路径仍然重要。
    Knowing a problem is solved motivates faster rediscovery, not despair; the path still matters.
  • 解决公理（AI能解决人类与AI合作能解决的任何问题）可能为假；人类探索更丰富。
    The solved axiom (AI can solve anything humans can with AI) may be false; human exploration is richer.
  • AI的证明可能不像人类的，但这是半人马数学的机会，而非恐惧。
    AI proofs may not look human, but that's an opportunity for centaur math, not dread.
  • 形式化解决了验证但未解决理解；我们需要故事来 grasp 证明。
    Formalization solves verification but not understanding; we need stories to grasp proofs.
  • 纳维-斯托克斯爆炸证明在数学上重要，但对工程实践无关紧要。
    The Navier-Stokes blow-up proof is mathematically important but practically irrelevant for engineering.

全文 · Full transcript（中英对照）

引言与AI数学突破 Introduction and the AI Math Breakthrough

Host

好的，这是一期紧急播客。我认为可以公平地说，关于数学和人工智能的现状，这是数学史上的一个危机时刻。过去几周，机器声称解决了数学史上一些最难的问题：纳维-斯托克斯方程、准黎曼假设、唯一游戏猜想，以及数百个其他结果。所以，今天和我在一起的有 Steven Brunton，他是数学家、华盛顿大学的流体动力学专家，可以谈论纳维-斯托克斯、湍流、所有流体相关的问题，以及许多其他话题。还有 Ankur Moitra，麻省理工学院的数学家和理论计算机科学家，今年在普林斯顿高等研究院访问，可以谈论许多事情，包括唯一游戏猜想和复杂性理论，以及人工智能如何学习的数学和所有理论计算机科学。还有 Alex，罗格斯大学的数学家、数论专家，也可以谈论非常多的话题，包括黎曼假设和 Lean、自动证明和自动验证检查等等，以及数学中的许多其他话题。三位都很出色。他们都非常友善，这么早起来和我交谈。我组装了这个多智能体的小型人类大脑集群，试图解读发生了什么。在这个时刻，许多数学家，包括陶哲轩和其他杰出的数学家，都感到惊慌，混合着敬畏、恐惧、 dread，还有一丝希望的微光。所以，我很抱歉以一个关于感受的问题开始，但你对这个历史性时刻有什么感受？也许，Alex，你想先来？

All right, so this is a kind of emergency episode. I think it's fair to say about the state of mathematics and artificial intelligence, a kind of crisis moment in the history of mathematics. Over the last few weeks, machines claimed to have solved some of the hardest problems in the history of math: Navier-Stokes, the quasi Riemann hypothesis, the unique games conjecture, and hundreds of other results. So, here with me, we have Steven Brunton, who's a mathematician, fluid dynamics expert at University of Washington, who can speak to Navier-Stokes, turbulence, all things fluid, and many other topics. Also here is Ankur Moitra, mathematician and theoretical computer scientist at MIT, visiting the Institute for Advanced Studies in Princeton this year, who can speak to many things, unique games conjecture and complexity theory in general, and the mathematics of how the AI learns and all things theoretical computer science. And Alex, a mathematician, number theorist at Rutgers, who can speak to also very many topics including Riemann hypothesis and Lean, the automated proof and automated verifying and checking and so on and so many other topics in mathematics. All three are brilliant. All are very kind to show up this early morning and talk to me. I assembled this kind of multi-agent mini swarm of human brains to try to interpret what happened. In this very moment, a lot of mathematicians, brilliant mathematicians including Terence Tao and others are freaking out, some mix of awe, terror, dread, and few inklings of light of hope, also. So, I apologize to start with a question about feeling, but what have you been feeling about this historic moment? Maybe, Alex, do you want to go first?

Alex

是的，我不知道我会不会称之为危机。危机中有很多机会。这是一个重新思考我们试图实现什么的机会。正是机会的混乱，有些人对此反应消极，有些人反应积极，有时这些反应发生在同一个人身上。所以，我认为我们正在进入，如果不是已经进入的话，某种数学知识的黄金时代，在几千年来我们拿着一根蜡烛在树林里行走，只能看到这么远之后。现在，我们有了手电筒。

Yeah, I don't know if I will call it a crisis. There's a lot of opportunity in that crisis. It's a chance to rethink what it is we're trying to accomplish. And it's the chaos of opportunity that some people are reacting negatively to, some people are reacting positively to, and sometimes those reactions are happening in the same person. And so, I think we're entering, if we're not already in, a period of some kind of golden age of mathematical knowledge after millennia of having a candle that we are walking around the woods, and this is as far as we can see. Now, we have a flashlight.

Host

我认为我们应该为不了解的人评论一下这个时刻的重要性。所以，也许引用你的话，类似‘天哪，如果人类做到这个，会立即获得菲尔兹奖。’也许你没说天哪，但基本上是说这是一个不可思议的结果。所以，很多人不会预料到在这么短的时间内取得这么大的进展。所以，这确实令人震惊。所以，你现在是作为一个在过去几天发现这件事后处理自己感受的人说话。所以，也许回到你第一次看到OpenAI发布的报告时的感受会很有用。你们俩碰巧同一天在镜头前，可能因为会议或聚会喝了几杯。也许，Ankur，你能说说你当时的感受吗？

I think we should comment on the magnitude of this moment for people who are not aware. So, maybe to quote you, something like, 'holy [expletive], if a human did this, it would be an instant Fields Medal.' Maybe you didn't say holy, but basically saying this is an incredible result. So, a lot of people wouldn't have expected this amount of progress in this amount of time. So, this is truly shocking. So, you're now speaking as a person who is dealing with the feelings you felt over the last couple of days having found this out. So, maybe also it'd be useful to go back to the feeling you were having when you first saw the report that in this case OpenAI dropped. And the two of you happened to be on camera loosely on the same day. Maybe a few drinks in because it was a conference or get-together. Maybe, Ankur, can you speak to what you were feeling?

Ankur

结果发布的那天晚上很有趣。现在，这并不完全出乎意料，因为几周来一直有关于我所在领域两个最大问题的传言，它们就在那批结果中。所以，唯一游戏猜想和 RL 等于 L。甚至有关于矩阵乘法的传言。所以，我最初的反应是哇，都是真的。谢天谢地终于出来了，因为现在人们可以真正去研究它，处理发生了什么，并弄清楚下一步该做什么。现在，关于你之前的问题，我们感觉如何？嗯，对我来说，这每天都在变化，但我认为最首要的感受是真正感觉被拉向两个非常不同的方向。我的工作一直是关于提出正确的问题。从这个意义上说，这些工具对我来说很棒，因为它们增加了我学习其他领域的能力。就像有一个世界专家在那里用你自己的语言教你东西。你建立联系的能力被放大了。但是，你知道，我工作中我最喜欢的部分之一是指导方面。不仅仅是培训研究生和博士后，看着他们做伟大的事情，而是让他们和我一起踏上这些旅程。所以，把我拉向另一个方向的部分是认识到我们长久以来的成功剧本和蓝图现在被抛到窗外了。所以，建议学生和博士后应该做什么工作才能产生影响、成名，变得更加困难。所以，我感到非常兴奋，但也许在智力上比以前更孤独。部分原因是很多时候伟大的结果来自提出非常谦逊的问题。至少对我来说是这样。现在有太多的焦虑和恐惧，感觉哇，你必须做出真正重大的事情才能在 AI 结果的海洋中脱颖而出。感觉我们失去了一些耐心，去播下种子，让伟大的橡树生长。

It was an interesting night when the results dropped. Now, it was not entirely unexpected because for weeks there'd been rumors about the two biggest problems in my areas that were in that collection of results. So, the unique games conjecture and RL equals L. There are even rumors about matrix multiplication. So, really my first reaction was wow, it's all true. Thank goodness it's finally out there because now people can actually go through it and process what's happened and figure out what to do next. Now, as to your earlier question, you know, what are we feeling? Well, for me it changes on a daily basis, but I think the biggest most overarching feeling is really feeling like you're getting pulled in two very different directions. My work has always been about asking the right questions. And to that extent, these tools are amazing for me because they increase my ability to learn about other fields. It's like having a world expert who's there to teach you about things in your own language. Your ability to make connections is just magnified. But, you know, one of the parts of my job that I really love the most is the mentorship aspect. Not just training grad students and postdocs and seeing them do great things, but having them come with me on these journeys. So, the part that pulls me in the other direction is the recognition that the playbook and the blueprint for success that we had for so long is now thrown out the window. So, it becomes a lot harder to advise students and postdocs about what are things they should be working on that are going to make an impact, are going to make a name for themselves. So, I feel very excited, but maybe more intellectually lonely than I felt before. In part because a lot of times great results come from asking very humble questions. At least for me. And now there's so much anxiety and dread and feeling that wow, you would have to do something really momentous to stand out in the sea of AI results. That it feels like we've lost some of that patience in planting the seeds from which the great oak trees grow.

Host

菲尔兹奖得主陶哲轩写道：‘我感到恐惧，许多人数年的耐心和刻意缓慢的研究努力，尤其是研究生和博士后，针对许多数学的激励性问题，被这样的发布随意扰乱或摧毁。就像一个人无法忘记电影剧透或填字游戏线索一样，一旦知道现有解决方案，就无法再那么有益和丰富地探索问题，我哀悼未走的道路和在开发技术的疯狂竞赛中失去的机会。’所以，我的意思是，如果现实一点，对博士生和博士后来说，这比你描述的更戏剧性。我是说，你说得相当平静，但如果我现在是博士生，我会处于震惊状态。就像，我一生都在经历这些。我知道很多朋友是程序员。去年十二月他们经历了这种震惊状态，基本上大多数编程世界转向 AI 编写大部分程序。所以，我会把它近似为一种失去意义的感觉，一种恐惧，黑暗的东西。我们会继续探索。Steve，你怎么想？你感觉如何？

Terence Tao, Fields Medal winner, wrote, 'I am horrified by the many person-years of ongoing patient and deliberately slow research efforts, particularly by graduate students and postdocs, toward many motivating problems of mathematics being casually disrupted or destroyed by such a release. Much as one cannot unhear a movie spoiler or a crossword clue, one cannot explore a problem as profitably and richly once one is aware of an existing solution, and I mourn the path not taken and the opportunities lost in the frantic race to develop the technology.' So, I mean, if we're being realistic, it's more dramatic for a PhD student and a postdoc than maybe the way you've articulated. I mean, you're saying it quite calmly, but if I was a PhD student right now, I'd be in a state of shock. Like, my whole life I was going through some of this. I know a lot of my friends are programmers. They're going through this state of shock last December when basically most of the programming world switched to AI writing most of the programs. So, I would approximate it to a feeling of kind of a loss of meaning, kind of terror, like dark stuff. We'll keep exploring that. Steve, what do you think? What are you feeling?

Steven

我会说我的大部分感受是充满希望的。我认为我是一名工程师。我喜欢解决问题，使技术对人类更现实，比如人造心脏和飞机，而我们解决实际应用问题的能力将会变得好得多。

I would say most of my feelings are pretty hopeful. I think I'm an engineer. I like solving problems that make technology more realistic for humans, artificial hearts and airplanes, and our ability to solve real applied problems is going to get much much better.

不确定性及人类目的 Uncertainty and Human Purpose

Ankur

我们的范围和影响将会扩大，因此我对此非常乐观。但存在很多不确定性，我认为其中一些对意义、对失去控制、对被排除在我们都热爱参与的过程之外的恐惧，是有点可怕的。而且，如果未来 5 到 10 年出现两种路径之一——我们真的被其他智能所 dwarf，或者它趋于平稳，成为我们用于好奇心、用于人类解决问题的不可思议的工具——我想我们谁都不会感到惊讶。这种不确定性是巨大的。那是两个截然不同的世界。所以不确定性对人类来说是困难的。我认为对我们这些选择数学的人来说也是困难的。我们选择数学，大概是因为我们喜欢规则和形式主义，而不确定性让人不舒服。不过，我认为谦逊的一面非常重要。这是一个很好的机会，让我们记住我们为什么这样做。比如，人类的目标是什么？不是出名。不是获得菲尔兹奖。解决有趣的问题，帮助他人，保持好奇，只是想理解世界。这些真的是很好的东西，值得回归。比如，我们需要什么才能成为满足的人类？我认为这是一个非常有趣的时刻，让我们思考。我最近一直在思考这个问题。对年轻人来说更困难，对吧？比如，如何找到自己的路，但从某种意义上说，我实际上对他们比对我们更有信心。

Our scope and reach is going to increase, and so for that I'm extremely hopeful. But there is a lot of uncertainty, and I think some of this fear of meaning, of losing control, of just being kind of cut out of the process that we all love being a part of, it is a little scary. And I don't think any of us would be surprised at either of the two paths that might take place over the next 5 or 10 years, where we really are just dwarfed by other intelligence, or it kind of levels off and this becomes an incredible tool that we use for our curiosity, for our human problem-solving. And that uncertainty is massive. Those are two very different worlds. And so uncertainty is difficult for humans. I think it's also difficult for those of us who have chosen math. We chose math probably because we liked rules and formalism, and uncertainty is uncomfortable. I think the humility aspect is really important, though. This is a really good opportunity for us to remember why we're doing this. Like, what is human purpose? It's not to be famous. It's not to have a Fields Medal. Solving interesting problems, helping other people, being curious, just wanting to understand the world. Those are really good things to kind of get back. Like, what do we need to be satisfied humans? And I think it's a really interesting moment for us to think about. I've been thinking about it a lot lately. It is more difficult for young people, right? Figuring out how to find their way, but I actually have more faith in them than in us in a sense.

Host

你认为 Terry 说的有道理吗？当你知道一个问题已经被解决时，就更难有动力去探索那个空间，尤其是当问题被 AI 系统这样解决时？

Do you think there's something to what Terry says about it's harder to be motivated when you know a problem has been solved? To explore that space when a problem has been solved like this by an AI system?

Steven

是也不是。从某种意义上说，我非常同意路径和目的地一样重要。就像如果你去公路旅行，公路旅行通常和到达那里一样有趣。同时，如果我们发现另一个国家发明了传送或时间旅行，我保证我们会在未来 5 年内重新发明它。就像如果你知道这是可能的，并且你可以稍微逆向工程一下，我们会好奇，我们会深入其中，我们会弄明白。所以，知道有解决方案也是更快到达那里的巨大动力。

Yes and no. In the sense that I do very much agree that the path is as important as the destination. Like if you go on a road trip, the road trip is usually just as fun as getting there. Alex

是的，你说了两件事，我想把它们分开。一个是解决数学，另一个是摧毁数学。所以，让我们把解决说得精确些。解决数学是什么意思？如果你回想国际象棋引擎刚出现的时候，人们说：“哦，这会很棒。我们将下半人马棋。”所以，这是人类加计算机系统对抗另一个人和计算机系统。这持续了大约 10 秒钟，然后计算机变得如此之好，以至于如果你不做任何事，除了选择计算机推荐的最佳着法，你就会输。所以，人类在那游戏中的角色变成了，是的，接受第一步。是的，接受第一步。那不再有趣了。我们再也看不到多少半人马棋了，对吧？所以，事实上，我刚刚和一位名叫 Thomas Gobert 的同事进行了一次对话，我们将我们对未来可能样子的论点公理化。我愿意为了论证而接受的一个公理是我称之为“已解决公理”的东西。已解决公理说，让我们假设在某个未来时刻——我们现在远未到达那里，我也不期望我们一定会到达那里；我们会看到能力是否会在某处逐渐减弱。但让我们假设已解决公理，它说任何可以通过人类与 AI 互动解决的数学问题，也可以由 AI 单独解决。这就是已解决公理。所以那意味着，真的，人类没有什么新的事情可做——你可以对 AI 系统说，好吧，我有问题 X。我想思考问题 X，但我取得进展的唯一方式，我在问题 X 上取得的任何进展都只是按下按钮。AI 思考它。它不一定解决它。这并不意味着它会解决每一个问题。黎曼猜想还没有解决。好吧？那才是我真正想要的解决方案。我只是没想到在我有生之年会看到我们几天前看到的任何进展。所以，并不是 AI 系统在解决每一个问题。它们在解决每一个可以通过人类与 AI 互动解决的问题。这就是那个公理的意思。所以我说，如果我们曾经在那个世界里，那必然会摧毁数学吗？我强烈的论点是，不。我不认为它会摧毁数学。

Yeah, so, you said two things and I'd like to separate them. One is to solve math and the other is destroy math. So, let's make solve precise. What does solve math mean? If you think back to when chess engines first came out and people were like, "Oh, this is going to be amazing. We'll play centaur games." So, this is a human being plus computer system against another human being and a computer system. And that lasted like 10 seconds and then the computers got so good that if you did anything but choose the top move that the computer recommended, you would lose. And so the human being's role in that game became, yes, accept the first move. Yes, accept the first move. That's no longer fun. And we don't see many games of centaur chess anymore, right? And so, in fact, I just had a conversation with a colleague named Thomas Gobert where we axiomatized our argument for what the future might look like. And one of the axioms that I'm willing to even accept for the sake of argument is what I call the solved axiom. And the solved axiom says, let's assume at some future point—we're nowhere near there now, and I don't expect us to be there necessarily; we'll see where the capabilities taper off if they do. But let's assume the solved axiom, which says any mathematical problem that can be solved with human interacting with AI could also have been solved by AI alone. That's the solved axiom. So that would mean that really there's nothing new for a human being to—you can say to an AI system, okay, I have problem X. I want to think about problem X, but the only way that I will make progress, any progress that I'll make on problem X will just be press the button. The AI thinks about it. I just didn't think in my lifetime I would see any kind of progress towards it that we saw a few days ago. So, it's not that AI systems are solving every problem. They're solving every problem that could be solved by humans interacting with AI. That's what that axiom would be. So I'm saying, if we are ever in that world, does that necessarily destroy mathematics? And my strong argument is no. I don't think it destroys mathematics.

所以，我不认为数学，纯数学，不是工程数学，不是那些能治愈癌症和解决喷气机——每个人都拥有喷气背包或你想象的其他技术进步。纯数学，我们知道我们证明定理只是因为我们好奇。我不认为定理的解决方案以 1 和 0 的形式存在于数据中心对人类有价值。我认为纯数学的价值在于它活在人类的头脑中。

抽象在数学中的作用 The Role of Abstraction in Mathematics

Ankur

所以，为了达到同样的终点，也许我需要开发更通用、更可抽象的方法，这些方法不仅能帮你解决那一个问题，还能解释联系或你想进入的其他方向，对吧？所以，当你回顾自己写的论文时，总是很神奇，你能看到这些有时看不见的线索，它们连接不同领域的工作，因为开发理解问题的系统方法有助于你解决周围的问题，而不仅仅是得到一个简洁形式化的定理。

I think it depends on what you mean by threads. For me, the reason you prove results is so that you better understand the world around you. Mathematics to me is a vehicle for abstraction and understanding things. And when you have a major result, what's important is not just the truth of the statement but what you've learned on route to doing that. Usually people's first question is, 'Okay, you proved something fantastic, but how did you do it?' And then people, once they digest that new idea, that new definition, that new way of looking at things, there are lots of other problems nearby that fall. Sometimes we reason in very sloppy metaphors that help us conceptualize the world around us.

元认知与工具使用 Metacognition and Tool Use

Ankur

举个例子，对吧？很多时候当我研究一个问题时，我可能有一个更大的目标想要实现，但通常我做的是尝试找到最简单、最令人尴尬的未解决问题，我仍然不知道如何解决。那种把它消化成‘天哪，我们怎么会不知道这个？’的方面，感觉是在正确的轨道上，因为然后当我们能解决那个例子时，也许我们正在组装我们需要攻击其他问题的工具集。所以，我确实认为我们将不得不变得更加有意识地思考我们关心什么？从元认知的角度，我们如何使用这些工具？我们到底委托给它们什么，不委托给它们什么？我们真的处于起步阶段，我们只是边走边拼凑。

So, just as an example, right? A lot of times when I'm working on a problem, I might have some bigger goal that I want to accomplish, but usually what I do is I try to find the simplest, most embarrassing open problem that I still don't know how to solve. That aspect of digesting it down to something, 'Boy, how can we not know this?' It feels like it's on the right track, because then when we can solve that example, maybe we're assembling the set of tools we need to make attacks on other problems. So, I do think we're going to have to become a lot more deliberate about what do we care about? How do we use these tools from a metacognition standpoint? What exactly are we delegating to them and what are we not delegating to them? And we're really at the beginning stages, where we're just trying to piece it together as we go along.

Steven

一方面，寻找证明有点像探索这棵可能性之树。你可能基于你见过的其他证明有一些直觉，知道该往哪个方向走。也许我应该走这个分支。也许我应该走那个分支。但有时我们遇到树中分支因子非常高的点。我们就是不知道往哪走。我们甚至没有猜测如果我们走下去会发现什么。现在在这个半人马世界的数学中，我们突然有了其他智能可以互动。而如何使用那个工具来寻找美丽的理论，我认为将是具有挑战性和不断发展的，但重要的是不要忽视我们不仅仅关心定理这一事实。

On the one hand, looking for a proof is kind of like exploring this tree of possibilities. You might have some intuitions based on some other proofs you've seen about which directions to go in. Maybe I should go down this branch. Maybe I should go down this branch. But sometimes we encounter points in the tree which have really high branching factor. We just don't know which way to go down. We don't even have a guess about if we went down them, what would we discover. And now in this centaur world of math, we suddenly have something else intelligent that we can interact with. And what is the right way to use that tool to search for the beautiful theories, I think will be challenging and evolving, but it's important to not lose sight of the fact that we don't just care about theorems.

门卫谬误与数学 The Doorman Fallacy and Mathematics

Ankur

所以，我是一名数学家，一名理论计算机科学家，但我也一直在更广泛地阅读社会科学，只是为了接受所有正在发生的变化。有一件事真正印在我脑海里，我很喜欢，叫做门卫谬误，非常有趣。所以，这个想法是你把一项复杂的工作归结为它最简单、最可见的任务。那么，门卫做什么？我猜他们在人们进出时帮忙开门，对吧？当我们住在纽约时，我们真的很喜欢和我们的门卫聊天。他们是很好的人，但如果你把它简化成那个简单的工作，你会想，‘哦，我可以用一个感应人们进出的滑动门来代替他们。’但你错过了他们工作中增加很多价值且难以表达的所有方面。我认为这也是思考 AI 时代数学的好方法。数学家做什么？工作中最简单、最可见的部分是我们证明定理。而关于数百年来未解决的猜想的定理，比如费马大定理，这些是非常可见的东西，我们花了很多时间普及它们，但我不认为这就是数学家的全部。我喜欢这样想：数学家是深入思考的人，深入思考的副产品是证明旧猜想，但也是建立新理论，建立看待旧事物的新方式，建立新的概念联系。

So, I'm a mathematician, I'm a theoretical computer scientist, but I've also been reading a little bit more broadly about social sciences just to sort of make my peace with all of the changes that have been happening. One of the things that really stuck in my mind that I liked is there's something called the doorman fallacy, which is very interesting. So, the idea is that you take a complex job and you boil it down to its simplest, most visible task. So, what does a doorman do? I guess they hold the door for people as they're coming and going, right? And when we lived in New York, we really enjoyed talking to our doorman. They were wonderful people, but if you digest it down to just that simple job, you can think, 'Oh, I can replace them just by a sliding door that detects when people are going or coming.' And you miss out on all the aspects of their job that add a lot of value and are harder to articulate. And I think that's a good way to think about mathematics in the age of AI, too. What does a mathematician do? The simplest and most visible part of the job is we prove theorems. And theorems about conjectures that were open for hundreds of years, like Fermat's Last Theorem, these are very visible things that we spend a lot of time popularizing, but I don't think that that's all that a mathematician does. I like to think about it as a mathematician is someone who thinks deeply, and a byproduct of thinking deeply is proving old conjectures, but it's also building new theories, building new ways of looking at old things, making new conceptual connections.

门卫谬误与人类工作价值 The Doorman Fallacy and the Value of Human Work

Host

而这些部分，你知道，也许 AI 也会吞掉。但我们现在还没到那一步。所以，尽管这是一个重大时刻，我不认为现在是按下恐慌按钮的时候。我认为现在是真正重新聚焦我们价值观的时候，并作为一个领域进行这些对话。记住，我们所做的事情确实有多元的美学和目标。当一件事变得廉价，比如解决定义明确的问题，它会让与之正交的其他事情变得更有价值。

And these are parts that, you know, maybe AI will swallow up, too. But we're not there yet at this moment. So, even though this is a momentous time, I don't think it's the right time to hit the panic button. I think it's the right time to really recenter in terms of what our values are and have these conversations as a field. And remember that what we do really has a plurality of different aesthetics and goals. And that when one thing becomes cheap, like problem-solving for well-defined things, it makes the other things that are orthogonal to it all that much more valuable.

That is really beautiful. The doorman fallacy going to perplexity here is the mistake of reducing a person's job to its most obvious task and assuming that automating that task replaces the value of the whole role. A hotel decides, quote, "We're paying someone to open the door, and automatic door can do that more cheaply." But, in fact, the doorman also welcomes guests and recognizes returning customers, handles luggage and hails taxis, provides security and discourages unwanted visitors, and signals prestige and makes guests feel cared for.

我会退一步说，在回答问题和解决问题之前，是提出正确的问题。所以，我认为衡量好的问题解决能力的方式是提出正确的子问题，提出一个有趣、可解决且可解释的问题。因此，我觉得提出好问题的价值一直是我们看重同事的品质，而且这会变得越来越有价值。

I would go one step behind answering questions and doing problem-solving is asking the right questions. So, like that is how I think you measure good problem-solving is asking the right sub-questions, asking, you know, a question that is interesting and can be solved and can be interpreted. And so, I feel like the value of of asking good questions is has always been something we value in our colleagues, and that's going to become increasingly more valuable.

I hope not. Um because, you know, really what stands out is uh having ideas that get adopted by your community that end up becoming powerful over time. They're trying to absorb what are the techniques in their field, what are the important problems, and they're trying to figure out how to make a name for themselves. And we used to do is we would give them a blueprint that was very simple to follow. Take a problem people care about and improve it. And then people will know who you are, you know, you'll get papers in top venues, you know, doors will open for you. 物理定律的自动发现 Automated Discovery of Physical Laws

Host

So, there are absolutely research programs that are systematically, automatically discovering minimal physical laws. So this is less mathematical theorems, more physics — governing laws and equations.

Steven

也就是观察自然系统，试图看看这些自然系统里是否存在简单的物理定律，能建模它们如何运作。

So, observing natural systems and trying to see in those natural systems if there are simple physical laws that model how they operate.

Host

对，把它提炼到最基础的部分。

Yeah, distilling it down to the basics.

Steven

这样你就能重现已知的理论。如果你观察球下落，你能推导出引力和 F 等于 MA。如果你观察流体，你能推导出纳维-斯托克斯方程。然后把这套方法用到我们还没写下简单方程的数据上——天气、湍流、太阳耀斑之类的东西。我猜数学里也在发生类似的事，那里大概也有这些可以被推导出来的最简片段。

So you can kind of recapitulate known theories. If you observe balls dropping, you can deduce gravity and F equals MA. If you observe fluids, you can deduce Navier-Stokes. And then taking that to data where we haven't written down simple equations — weather, turbulence, solar flares, things like that. And I'm assuming similar things are happening in math, where there are probably these minimal pieces that can be deduced.

Alex

所以，我对“已解决公理”的理解是：我作为人类数学家做的任何事——我好几次坐下来想，“好吧，LLM 永远做不了我做的这件事。”然后我就想，“嗯，为什么不能？”我可以问一个问题。我可以问它一个问题。我可以问它，“我应该问哪些问题？”

Host

好，你相信“已解决公理”吗？在我们能多接近它的这个谱系上，你觉得我们在哪儿？在这个谱系里，你认为人类的角色是什么？

Okay, do you believe in the solved axiom? Where on the spectrum of how close we can get to it? And in that spectrum, what's the role of the human, do you think?

Steven

我想我并不真的相信“已解决公理”。我觉得它在某些方面也许对了一半，而弄清楚哪一半是对的，会非常有趣也非常有挑战。所以我认为在 AI 能解决什么这件事上，还会有重大惊喜和更多进展。但对我来说，当你面对一项非常非常强的新技术时，危险在于：当你试图去概念化它为什么有效时，你会通过拟人化来理解它。所以这里较早的一个教训也许来自深度学习。有一些很惊人的例子——你可以用神经网络做到人类水平甚至更好的物体识别。但总体而言，它成功的方式不如人类那样稳健。因为存在对抗样本这类例子：你可以给自然图像加上非常细微的图案，就能完全骗过物体检测系统，却骗不过人。我在 MIT 有位前同事，Aleksander Madry。他们做过一些很好的工作，我很喜欢。我记得标题大概是《对抗样本是特征，不是缺陷》。他们做了下面这个很酷的实验。你拿一堆自然数据，也许是猫和狗的图片。你训练一个深度网络把它们识别得很好。你给它们加上一些对抗扰动，让——在 AI 系统看来——猫看起来像狗，反之亦然。所以它就像一个小小的图案，改变了物体检测器的工作方式。然后你造一个完全虚假的数据集。里面有猫的图片，上面叠着狗的图案，你把它标成狗，猫则反过来。神奇的是，当你用这些全错的标签训练一个深度网络时，它会学到在自然数据上表现很好的东西。所以，不知怎么的，当我们识别猫和狗时，我们是以一种能用腿、尾巴和整体形状来表述的方式去做的。但很多时候，当深度网络或 AI 能做事时，它们可能会找到解决问题的暗门或后门，利用数据中大量人类不用的统计关系。所以对我来说，重要的是不要在你所能做的和 AI 不能做的之间划出非常鲜明的界限，因为你会出错。但这并不意味着我不相信会出现某种界限，哪怕只是因为我认为我们过度拟人化了它们解决问题的方式。而当我翻看 OpenAI 的解答时，它们看起来就不像是人类解出来的。所以对我来说，似乎仍然存在非常大的质的差异，而这其实是合作和半人马数学的机会，而不是对 AI 霸主的恐惧。不过，谁知道呢？下周可能就错了。

I guess I don't really believe in the solved axiom. I think maybe it's half true in some ways, and figuring out which half of it is true will be very interesting and challenging. So I think there'll be major surprises and more to come in terms of what AI can solve. But for me, when you're facing a new technology that's really, really good, the danger is that when you try to conceptualize why it's working, you do so by anthropomorphizing it. So maybe one of the earlier lessons here is from deep learning. So there are these amazing examples — you can take neural nets and you can do human-level or better object recognition. But in general, the way that it succeeds is less robust than how a human does. Because there are all these examples like adversarial examples, where you can add very slight patterns to natural images that can completely fool the object detection systems, but not a human. And I had a former colleague at MIT, Aleksander Madry. They had some really nice work, which I really enjoyed. I think the title was something like "Adversarial Examples Are Features, Not Bugs." So they did the following cool experiment. You take a bunch of natural data, maybe pictures of cats and dogs. You train a deep network to identify them very well. You add some adversarial perturbations to them that make — to the AI system — a cat look like a dog and vice versa. So it's kind of like a small little pattern that changes how the object detector works. And then you create a totally fake data set. It's got pictures of cats with dog patterns on top, and you label it as dog, and vice versa for cats. And amazingly, when you train a deep network on this with all the wrong labels, it's going to learn something that does well on natural data. So somehow when we recognize what a cat and dog is, we do it in a way that we can articulate in terms of its legs and its tail and its shape in general. But a lot of times when deep networks or AI can do things, they might be able to find trapdoors or backdoors to solving the problem that use a lot of statistical relationships in the data that a human doesn't. So to me, it's important to not draw this boundary very sharply between what you can do and what AI can't do, because you're going to be wrong. But that doesn't mean that I don't believe there will emerge some kind of boundary, if only for the fact that I think we over-anthropomorphize the way that they solve problems. And when I'm looking through the OpenAI solutions, they just don't look like a human has solved those problems. So to me, there still seems to be a very big qualitative difference, and that's an opportunity really for collaboration and centaur math more than it is dread about the AI overlords. But who knows? Could be wrong next week.

Host

你的直觉是，这其中有多少是 AI 只是把成千上万篇不同的论文串起来、顺着线索去拉——抱歉又用回这个比喻——但就是不同论文的线索，然后基本上只是把不同论文里已经存在的证明表述出来，做一些小的连接？还是它真的在发展新的数学思想？你有没有看到那种天才般的 AlphaGo 围棋“第 37 手”的微光？

What's your intuition about how much of this is AI just tying together a bunch of thousands of disparate papers and pulling at the threads — sorry for bringing back the metaphor — but the threads of the different papers, and then just basically articulating the proofs that were already there in the different papers and you're making minor connections? Or is it actually developing new mathematical ideas? Have you seen glimmers of whatever that genius AlphaZero move in the game of Go?

Alex

对，第 37 手。

Yeah, move 37.

Host

第 37 手。

Move 37.

Alex

我还没读完他们全部 700 篇论文。我看过的那些——有些人对此很不屑，说，“哦，那都在凸包里面。你做人已知的那些动作，只是做得非常好，做了很好的树搜索。”我不会把它叫作凸包。我觉得 Tim Gowers 有个很好的说法，把它称作由所有这些生成的一个半群。是的，它还是把已知动作组合起来的概念，但已知动作的空间、它能达成的东西，比那种带贬义的凸包要大得多得多。所以就准黎曼猜想而言，它走的那些步都是人类已知的东西。而其中有很长的一连串，当你看着它时会感到震惊，说，“天哪，太美了。你怎么想到——当然，我是在拟人化——你怎么想到在这一步之后走那一步，再之后走那一步？”是的，就是这样。

So I haven't read all 700 of their papers. The ones that I have looked at — some people speak about this dismissively, like, "Oh, it was in the convex hull. You do the moves that we know as humans and you just do them really well and do a really good tree search." I wouldn't call it the convex hull. I think Tim Gowers had a really good way of talking about it instead as the semigroup generated by all of these things. Yes, it's the same notion of putting together known moves, but the space of known moves, what it can accomplish, is much, much bigger than this kind of derogatory convex hull. So in terms of the quasi-RH, the moves that it took are all things that were known to human beings. And there's a very long cascade of them that is kind of shocking when you look at it and say, "Oh my god, that's so beautiful. How did you think to — I'm anthropomorphizing it, of course — how did you think to do this move after that move and after that move?" And yeah, that does it.

凸包与半群直觉 Convex Hull vs. Semigroup Intuition

Host

你能向普通大众解释一下直觉吗？半群和凸包之间的界限，那个带有贬义的凸包，当然还有准黎曼猜想，那是重大证明之一？

Could you articulate to the more general public the intuition, the line between semi-group and convex hull, the derogatory convex hull, and the quasi RH, of course, is the quasi Riemann hypothesis that one of the big proofs?

Ankur

好，我试着这样解释。你想象一个气球。气球内部就是它的凸包。所以我们有所有这些结果，所有数学家想出来的这些想法。然后里面的任何东西都是它们的某种线性组合。但它感觉是紧致的。就像有一个封闭的区域，而在那个区域内，AI 并没有真正取得新进展，因为它没有把气球往外推。它还没有给我们一个新想法。

Yeah, let me try to do it like this. You have Think of like a balloon. So, the inside of the balloon is its convex hull. And so, we have like all of these results that all these ideas that mathematicians have thought up. And then anything inside of that is some linear combination of it. But it feels compact. It's like there's this kind of closed enclosed area, and within that area, like AI hasn't really made new progress cuz it hasn't moved the balloon out. It hasn't given us a new idea yet.

Ankur

说它带有贬义，是因为那里的直觉是，我们本可以做到，数学家本可以做到，因为它已经相当明显。它已经在已经思考和发展的空间之内了。

And it's derogatory because the intuition there is we could have done it mathematician could have done it because it's already quite obvious. It's already within the the space of things that have already been thought about and developed.

Steven

是的，它读过了每一篇论文。这正是 Ankur 刚才说的。它读过了每一篇论文。它把所有这些都内化了。这比任何人类大脑能容纳的还要多，但人类有时间去阅读、学习和内化。所以，在广度上，是的，它击败了任何人类。然后在深度上，它真的产生了一个真正的新想法吗？你可以争论，至少在准黎曼猜想的情况下，我不会说它是一个真正的新想法。它是许多非常著名事物的美丽组合。有很多人正在懊悔，他们 20 年前就有过那个方向的想法。两年前，在三次 theta 函数和三次高斯和上有一个重大突破，最终成为了 AI 通向解决方案的路径。但为了与半群对比，我把半群想象成像瓷砖。所以，你只是在棋盘上玩瓷砖，你被允许做的是，每当你有一块瓷砖，你可以把另一块瓷砖放在左边、右边、前面或后面。然后你就可以开始铺东西了。这就像半群作用。问题是，你能达到多远？你可以走，我不知道，你可以用现有想法走得非常非常远，可能证明比我们人类能够探索的多得多。

Yeah, so it has read every paper. It's exactly what Ankur was just saying. It has read every paper. It has internalized all of that. That's more than any human brain not even could hold, but has time to read and learn and internalize. So, in terms of breadth, yes, it beats any human being. And then in terms of depth, you know, has it really generated a genuinely new idea? And you can argue that at least in the case of quasi RH, I would not say it's a genuinely new idea. It's a It's a beautiful combination of a lot of things that were very well known. There are a lot of people who are kicking themselves that had some idea of in that direction 20 years ago. And and two years ago there was a big breakthrough on cubic theta functions and cubic Gauss sums and you know, that ended up being that the path that the AI took to to the solution. But so to contrast that with a semigroup, I think of a semigroup as like tiles. So you you know, you just playing tiles on the board and what you're allowed to do is whenever you have a tile you can put another tile to the left, to the right, to the front or to the back. And and then you can just start tiling things. And that's like a semigroup action. You and and the question is well, how far can you reach with that? You can go I don't know, you know, you can go really really far out with with existing ideas might prove a whole lot more than we as humans have been able to explore.

Host

所以你的直觉更倾向于半群方向，意思是至少有一丝扩张的迹象，而不是凸包内的线性组合。

So your intuition is more in the semigroup direction, meaning like there is at least glimmers of expansion versus linear combinations inside the convex hull.

Steven

你知道，我们如何选择解释它正在做的事情，我认为取决于我们，但在整个数学史上，有一些看似非常困难和深刻的问题，然后有人想出了一个三页的解决方案，非常初等，本可以向本科生解释，并且本可以在问题首次提出时的 20 年前被发现，如果它在 20 年前被发现，它就会是某本教科书中的练习。因为那个解决方案没有被发现，而真正聪明的人研究了很长时间并取得了渐进进展，使这个问题非常出名，所以当它真正被解决时，它就成了一个大事件。这在数学中时不时发生。一年前我写的时候说过，所以今年夏天有一个国际数学家大会，我在写我的会议录，他们要求提前 10 个月。所以我说，你知道，我现在要写关于数学和 AI 的什么，10 个月后仍然相关？我认为那里的内容有些常青。我坚持我所写的。但是，我在那里写的一件事正是，我们将发现，更多我们认为当前技术无法解决的问题，实际上可以通过当前技术解决，如果你能以正确的方式组合起来。这就是 AI 擅长的地方。再次，有人可以解释为，哦，所以它在里面，这些解决方案在凸包内。你想这么称呼，没问题。我认为那并非无效。

同行评审与数学真理 Peer Review and Mathematical Truth

Steven

所以，数学期刊在 AI 出现之前就已经因为数量问题而承受着非常非常严重的压力。与 AI 无关，只是有更多的人产生更多的知识，发送给期刊，期刊必须进行审稿，审稿人筋疲力尽，被要求非常努力地理解这些定理并检查它们。我认为，唯一比那个系统更糟糕的事情就是没有那个系统。因为我们的数学认识论，我们达到真理的方式，是故意缓慢、仔细和精确的。你可以回顾佩雷尔曼对庞加莱猜想的解决方案。他把这些论文放在 arXiv 上。然后世界各地的人都被震惊了。所以他们邀请他来做讲座。他来到石溪，来到纽约大学，来到普林斯顿。他走遍全国和世界。他做 3 小时长的即兴解释，也许一个小时是他解释他在做什么，接下来的两个小时是人们说：“在第 247 页，引理 412，我不明白你是怎么做的。”然后他走到黑板前，给他们做一个半小时的讲座，讲解他是怎么做的。所以整个事情活在他的头脑中。他已经尽力在纸上沟通。然后由我们社区来非常仔细、非常详细和精确地回顾，并同意：“是的，这是庞加莱猜想的真正证明。”这花了数年时间和大量的人力。所以提出解决方案只是过程的第一步。这个过程必须被交付，并且必须由社区驱动。这就是期刊系统试图实现的目标。

So, journals were already in math, uh, under very, very severe strain from the number of things before AI. Having nothing to do with AI, just there was, you know, more people producing more knowledge, sending it to journals, and journals had to referee it, and referees are exhausted and being asked to, uh, work really hard to understand these theorems and check them. Uh, the only thing worse than that system, I think, is not having that system. Because the way that like our mathematical epistemology, the way that we arrive at truth, is deliberately slow and careful and precise. You go back to Perelman's solution of the Poincaré conjecture. He puts these papers on arXiv. And then people around the world are, you know, shocked by this. And so they invite him to come give lectures. He gives He comes to Stony Brook. He comes to NYU. He comes to Princeton. He's going all around the country and the world. And he's giving 3-hour-long impromptu explanations of of, you know, maybe an hour is is him explaining what he's doing and the next 2 hours are people saying, "On page 247, lemma 412, I don't understand how you do this." And then he goes to the board and gives them a half-hour lecture on how he does that. So the entire thing lives in his mind. He's done the best he can to communicate it on paper. And then it's up to us, the community, to go back through it very carefully, very in a detailed and precise way, and agree, "Yes, this is a real proof of the Poincaré conjecture." And that took years to do and a whole lot of human effort. So coming up with the solution is only the first step in the process. And that process has to be delivered and it has to be community driven. And so that's what the journal system is trying to accomplish.

In many other fields, we have moved past the point where we can't individually advance, you know, circuit design or aircraft design or protein folding or any you know, astronomy. And again, I'm an engineer, so I'm thinking leadership is obviously the thing that a community needs when it's having this kind of crisis of organization and individuality moving towards a more collective model. Um, and that's interesting because I know so many mathematicians and like by kind of organization, this is a very independent group of people who don't like being told what to do, who don't like being told what to study, who are very independent by their very nature. Um, you know, we like solitude and working alone. That's why we, you know, spend so much time doing it.

用Lean和MathLib形式化数学 Formalizing Mathematics with Lean and MathLib

Host

As a quick aside, can you mention what MathLib is and Lean just because we mentioned it a few times for folks?

Steven

当然。所以数学理想上是一个形式化过程，从公理化系统开始，然后从那里进行逻辑推理规则。所以如果人们想到——我不知道，人们还在高中学习欧几里得几何吗？那应该是孩子们第一次看到证明和公理结构的模型。

So if people think of—I don't know, do people still learn Euclid's geometry in high school or something? That's supposed to be the kind of model for kids to start seeing proofs for the first time and seeing an axiomatic structure.

Host

嗯。

Mhm.

Steven

你可以把这个想法写进软件，说这里是数学的实际公理，这里是逻辑推理规则，而 Lean——这类软件有很长的历史，叫做交互式定理证明器，你在证明中逐行编写，计算机说是的，这一行确实从上一行的目标状态通过逻辑推理得出。所以，Lean 是允许你检查各种东西的软件。它可以检查你代码的规范是否符合你试图定义的内容。这就是它被发明的原因——为了无 bug 的代码，希望未来我们能有无 bug 的代码，因为 AI 系统非常擅长发现人类编写的代码中的 bug。所以，对于那个应用来说，它变得越来越重要。但另一个应用是检查数学结果。并允许你以非常正式的方式编写数学结果。所以，现在 Lean 本身只是检查逻辑步骤的硬件。你不想每个数学项目都从定义自然数开始，使用零和后继——你知道，自然数的皮亚诺公理。所以，有一个大型的通用库，维护得非常好，组织得非常好，等等，是很好的。这就是 MathLib。MathLib 是 Lean 生态系统中用于形式化数学的 Lean 库。再次，Lean 只是一个交互式定理证明器，还有很多其他的。它是到目前为止在数学战争中获胜的那个，如果有数学战争的话——这是两个人的竞争之类的。

And you can take that idea and write it in software and say here are the actual axioms of mathematics, here are the rules of logical inference, and Lean—there's a long history of this kind of software, it's called interactive theorem provers, where you're writing line by line in a proof, and the computer says yes, this line does follow by logical inference from the goal state on the previous line. So, Lean is software that allows you to check all kinds of things. It can check the specifications of your code align with what you were trying to define. That's what it was invented for—was bug-free code, and hopefully in the future we will have bug-free code because AI systems are really good at finding bugs in code written by humans. So, it's becoming ever more important for that application. But another application is to check mathematical results. And allow you to write a mathematical result in a very formal way. And so, now Lean itself is just the hardware that checks the logical steps. You do not want to start every math project by defining what the natural numbers are, using zero and the successor—you know, the Peano axioms of the natural numbers. So, it's nice to have a large general library that's extremely well-maintained, extremely well-organized, and so on. And that's what MathLib is. MathLib is the Lean library for formalizing mathematics in the Lean ecosystem. And again, Lean is just one interactive theorem prover, there are lots of others. It's the one that so far has been winning the math wars, if there are any math wars—it's a competition of two people or something.

That's right. And so, the fact that they're saying things doesn't mean that they necessarily have a model of mathematics in their heads, whatever that means, the same way that we do. But that's okay, because they'll write a Lean code that builds on top of MathLib, builds on top of the prime number theorem plus project or whatever, and checks that it has indeed solved this problem down to the axioms.

逆向教授实分析 Teaching Real Analysis Backwards

Alex

头几节课讲的是实数到底是什么。我们得把它们定义或构造出来，比如有理数柯西序列的柯西等价类，天知道那都是什么意思。一门实分析入门课要花上两周才能讲到那儿。然后我们有了实数轴，现在终于可以开始做微积分了，用正式的 epsilon-delta 定义，诸如此类。这一直让我觉得很奇怪，因为上课之前我就知道实数是什么了。它就是这条数轴，然后我们花两周讲有理数的柯西序列，到了第三周，它又变回数轴了。这到底有什么意义？

The first couple of lectures are about what are the real numbers. We have to define them or construct them as Cauchy equivalence classes of Cauchy sequences of rationals, whatever the hell all that means. It takes like two weeks for a first-semester course in real analysis to get there. And then we have the real number line, and now we can start doing calculus with formal epsilon and delta definitions, yada yada. And it always struck me as bizarre that we do this, because before the class, I knew what the real numbers were. It's this number line, and then we spend these two weeks talking about Cauchy sequences of rationals, and then on week three, they're the number line again. What the hell was the purpose of that?

Alex

这是因为过去写东西的方式非常工程化。你得先构造出实数，然后才能开始用它们。但人类早就在用实数了，远早于我们意识到它们需要被形式化地构造出来。所以我在课上做的是直接跳到 epsilon-delta 微积分。我们做了六周的计算，直到某一天，等等，这里有个定理——它在实数里成立，涉及某种收敛性，叫波尔查诺-魏尔斯特拉斯定理。你有一个在固定区间里跳来跳去的实数序列，必然存在某个子序列收敛到一个实数。可这在有理数里不成立。为什么在实数里成立？实数到底是什么？所以，你不碰到一个需要解决的问题，就不会开始问这个问题。

And so, it's because the way things are written, or had been written, was very engineering. You need to construct the real numbers before you start using them. But human beings used the real numbers long before we understood that they needed to be constructed formally. So what I did in my class is I jumped straight into epsilon-delta calculus. We did six weeks of calculations until we got to a point where, wait a second, here's a theorem — it's true in the real numbers, there's some convergence, something called the Bolzano-Weierstrass theorem. You have a sequence of real numbers that are jumping around but in a fixed range; there has to be some subsequence that converges to a real number. Well, that's not true in the rationals. Why is it true in the reals? What are the reals? So you don't start asking that question until you hit a problem that needs to be solved.

Alex

所以我教这门课的方式，跟把它当成工程项目来教所需的方式正好相反，因为你不先把实数构造出来、不先知道它们是什么，就不能直接谈实数。如果你是在搭建理论，那没错；但如果你是在讲故事，那你完全可以就从实数开始。你对它是什么有直觉，直到你的直觉崩掉之前，你都不需要知道它究竟是怎么被构造出来的那个分辨率。

So the way I taught the course was sort of backwards from how it needs to be taught if it's an engineering project, because you can't just start talking about the real numbers until you've constructed them, until you know what they are. Well, that's correct if what you're doing is building up the theory, but if you're telling a story, then you absolutely can just start with the real numbers. You have an intuition for what that is, and until your intuition breaks, you don't need the resolution of how you actually construct them.

Alex

所以我认为我们未来写论文的方式会非常不同，我们会花更多时间讲这个过程的故事：我在这过程中学到了什么，什么是有意思的——这里、这里和这里你用的是标准做法，但到了这里你卡住了，你往哪儿走？这里有个想法，解释为什么你往左走而不是往右、往上、往下之类。而且有一个 Lean 形式化伴随着这个故事，让你知道工程上确实行得通。因为你不能光讲故事。如果只讲故事，数学不是——它不是音乐，音乐里你可以讲一个漂亮的故事，某处有平行五度、你破了某条规则也没关系，反正巴赫老这么干。

So I think the way that we write papers in the future is going to be very different, where we spend a lot more time just talking about the story of what have I learned in this process, what are the interesting — here you do the standard thing here, here, and here, but then here you're sort of stuck and where do you go? And here's an idea for why you go left and not right and not up and down and whatever. And there's a Lean formalization that accompanies this story that lets you know that the engineering actually works out. Because you can't just tell stories. If you just tell stories, math is not — it's not music, where you can tell a beautiful story and it's okay that you have parallel fifths moving somewhere and you've broken some rule, but Bach does it all the time, so.

数学作为故事与挣扎 Mathematics as Story and Struggle

Ankur

我觉得这一点非常重要，正好强调一下 Alex 说的。有一种关于数学的看法，我并不认同，就是把数学看成定理不断上涨的潮水。AI 怎么会不好呢？它在给这股潮水添砖加瓦。但数学真的不只是我们拥有多少条定理，远不止于此。它还关乎与问题搏斗的那个故事。因为当你和一个好问题搏斗时，它会反击你，你会从中有所领悟。它真正关乎的是你沿途想出的那套信念与抽象体系——当你和这个问题搏斗时，你会发现某种更普遍、适用更广的意义。我认为这是一个绝佳的机会，让我们摆脱那种“我们只是一直在公理化，直到抵达下一条定理”的想法。讲出我们究竟是如何走到这些问题上的故事、它们与其他问题有什么相似之处，在这个关键时刻，比以往任何时候都更重要。

So I think that's a very important point, just to emphasize the point that Alex is making. There's this view of mathematics, which I don't believe in, where it's just this rising tide of theorems. How can AI be bad? It's contributing to this rising tide. But really mathematics is not just about the number of theorems we have, far from it. It's also about that story of fighting with problems. Because when you fight with a good problem, it fights back at you and you learn something about it. It's really about the system of beliefs and abstractions you come up with en route — when you're fighting this problem, you discover some more general meaning that applies more broadly. And I think it's a great opportunity to disabuse ourselves of this idea that we're just axiomatizing things until we get to the next theorem. Telling that story of how we actually got to these problems, what the parallels are to other problems, is now, at this pivotal moment, more important than it ever has been.

Alex

是的。回到你那个问题，就是，这是不是规模的问题？嗯，是的，就是规模的问题，但正如 Ankur 所说，我们思考数学的方式关乎故事，而可能我们并不理解这件事的故事。你可以逐行跟着一个证明走，却不明白到底在发生什么。你也许可以叫另一个 LLM 给你讲讲这个故事，如果它能给出这样的解释的话。但那正是我们想要的。我们想要故事。我们想要理解。

Yes. To get all the way back to your question about, you know, is it a matter of scale? Well, yes, it's just a matter of scale, but exactly to Ankur's point, the way that we think about mathematics is about stories, and it might be that we don't understand the story of this. You can follow a proof line by line and not understand what's really going on. You might just ask some other LLM to explain to you the story, if it can come up with such an explanation. But that's what we want. We want stories. We want understanding.

Host

我们知道素数是什么。人们对素数的分布很感兴趣。而黎曼发现的是——这简直疯了——存在一个函数，它以复数为输入，以复数为输出。而这个函数支配着素数的行为。它叫黎曼 zeta 函数。所谓 zeta 函数的零点，指的是那些让输出为复平面上数字零的复数输入，也就是复平面的原点。问题就是，这些复零点在哪里？什么样的复数输入，在你代入这个 zeta 函数后，会得到零这个复数输出？

So we know what the prime numbers are. People are interested in the distribution of the prime numbers. And what Riemann discovered is that there's a function — this is totally crazy — that there's a function that takes complex numbers as inputs and gives complex numbers as outputs. And that function governs the behavior of the prime numbers. It's called the Riemann zeta function. And by the zeros of the zeta function, we mean complex inputs where the output is the number zero in the complex plane, so the origin in the complex plane. And the question is, where are these complex zeros? What complex number inputs give you a complex number output of zero when you apply this zeta function?

Alex

这到底为什么会——复数，你在说什么？我们有的是这些在数轴的自然数里、不能被任何其他数整除的离散数字，现在你却要谈从复空间到复空间的函数。这就是那个伟大的突破。伟大的突破在于弄明白了如何对素数做信号处理。你想想，人们能听到你的播客，是因为我的声带在产生声音的压力波。那是自然空间。然后它进入麦克风。

Why in the world should that — complex numbers, what are you talking about? We have these discrete numbers that aren't divisible by anything else sitting in the natural numbers on the number line, and now you want to talk about functions from a complex space to a complex space. That was the great breakthrough. The great breakthrough was to figure out how to do signal processing on the prime numbers. If you think about it, the reason people can hear your podcast is that my voice box is producing pressure waves of sound. That's the natural space. Then that goes into the microphone.

素数导论与黎曼猜想 Introduction to Prime Numbers and the Riemann Hypothesis

Host

麦克风对它进行分析，做傅里叶分析，把它转换成振幅和频率，就得到一堆数字，存储这些数字或发送这些数字，随便怎样。然后扬声器拿这些数字重建出在人们听来像我的声音。同样的事情也在发生。这就是 zeta 函数对质数所做的。它把质数——你可以把质数想象成空气中振动的分子颗粒——对它做信号处理分析，能够恢复出同样的质数。频率和振幅对应于 zeta 函数的零点。zeta 函数包含了那些信息。而黎曼假设——所有这些的振幅，你可以称之为黎曼波，这些波的叠加恢复了质数的分布。所有这些振幅都应该完全相同。就像演奏质数音乐的交响乐团，每件乐器都以完全相同的振幅发声。这就是黎曼假设所预测的。

The microphone analyzes it, does the Fourier analysis on it, converts it into amplitudes and frequencies, and it's just a bunch of numbers, stores those numbers or sends those numbers, whatever. The speaker then takes those numbers and reconstructs back out what sounds to people like my voice. The same thing is going on. That's what the zeta function does for the prime numbers. It takes the prime numbers—you can think of the prime numbers as particles of air molecules wiggling around—and it does a signal processing analysis on it that can recover the same prime numbers back. And the frequencies and the amplitudes correspond to zeros of the zeta function. The zeta function has that information in it. And the Riemann hypothesis—so the amplitudes of all of these, you can call them Riemann waves, and the superposition of those waves recovers the distribution of the primes. And all of those amplitudes are supposed to be exactly the same. Like the orchestra that plays the music of the prime numbers has every single instrument sounding at exactly the same amplitude. That's what the Riemann hypothesis predicts.

Host

Host

13 million lines, okay.

Steven

所以就在两天前，我在普林斯顿数学系做了一个学术报告，我详细解释了 Lean 是什么，MathLib 是什么，但也提到当今的 AI 系统——有这样一个 MathLib 库，其中一切都做得完美，恰好达到正确的泛化水平，不应该有任何重用代码——那是在其他地方重新证明定理。一切都是最一般、最抽象的。而 AI 系统能做的就是在上面构建一两层。但它们所做的是临时拼凑。它们还没有被训练——我不是说它们做不到，但它们现在不擅长——我称之为“典范化”的工作，即把有点粗糙的代码重构，准确找出它在库中属于哪里，这个引理的规范陈述版本是什么，然后每个证明都应该只是组合——你应用这里的这个引理，你应用那里的那个引理，把所有这些放在一起，就是你的定理。然后它非常干净，非常美丽，非常优雅。这就是 MathLib 追求的目标。我在报告中说的是，我们今天所处的位置是，你可以让整本教科书形式化，只要它们在 MathLib 的光环内，只要它们与 MathLib 中已经完成的东西相隔一两步。但当你开始——这曾是我的实践，我的经验是——当你开始要求它做与库相隔三四步的事情时，它为这个对象想出的定义方式，以及它为同一对象的另一个定义方式，因为它是为了两个不同目的而做的，然后它们冲突，你无法把这些东西重新拼凑起来。你真的需要 MathLib 库增长，才能达到可以写研究手稿的地步，真正利用综合大量不同数学领域。

So I just 2 days before I gave a colloquium in the Princeton math department where I explained in painstaking detail what Lean is, what MathLib is, but also that AI systems today—there's this MathLib library where everything is kind of perfectly done, exactly the right level of generality, and there's not supposed to be any code that's reusing code that's all—that's re-proving theorems somewhere else. Everything is the most general, the most abstract. And what AI systems are capable of doing is building like one or two layers on top of that. But what they're doing is jerry-rigging. They have not been trained yet—I'm not saying they can't do it, but they're not good at it right now—at what I call canonization, which is taking kind of sloppy code, refactoring it, out exactly where in the library it belongs, what's the canonical version of the statement of this lemma, and then every proof should just be a combination of—you apply this lemma from over here, you apply that lemma from over there, put all these things together, there's your theorem. And then it's really clean, it's really beautiful, very elegant. That's what MathLib is going for. And what I said in my colloquium is where we're at today is that you can get entire textbooks formalized as long as they're in the MathLib halo, as long as they're like one, maybe two steps removed from things that have already been done in MathLib. But when you start—and this had been my practice, my experience had been—when you start asking it to do things that are three or four steps removed from the library, the way that it came up with a definition of this object and the way that it came up with some other definition of the same object because it was doing it for two different purposes, then they clash, you can't stitch these things back together. You really need the library MathLib to grow in order to get to a place where you can do research manuscripts that are really using synthesizing tons of different areas of mathematics.

Host

这就是为什么你会预期费马大定理需要时间，因为它远远超出 MathLib 的光环，需要相当多的时间。

Which is why you would expect Fermat's Last Theorem to take because it's so far outside of the halo of MathLib, it would take quite a bit of time.

Steven

完全正确。所以，Kevin Buzzard 有一百万美元的资助，用于未来五年，或者我想他可能已经进行了一两年，将费马大定理的形式化归约到 1980 年代已知的东西。所以，他想完成最后一步，即怀尔斯工作以及怀尔斯-泰勒的那七年，最终完成费马大定理，但建立在那些我们知道会极其困难的东西之上。他不打算做 Langlands-Tunnell，不打算做 Mazur，有各种各样的事情预计比这五年要做的更困难得多。而我在那个报告中所说的一切都是错的。我想我的报告是在周三，周五就出现了一个 1300 万行代码的发布，从公理证明了费马大定理。

Exactly right. So, Kevin Buzzard had a million-dollar grant to spend the next five years, or I think maybe he's already a year or two into that grant, to reduce the formalization of Fermat's Last Theorem to things that were known in the 1980s. So, he wanted to do that last step, that those seven years of Wiles's work and Wiles-Taylor to finally finish Fermat's Last Theorem, but built on top of things that we knew would be extraordinarily difficult. He wasn't going to do Langlands-Tunnell, he wasn't going to do Mazur, there were all kinds of things that were expected to be even much more difficult than what these five years would take. And everything I said in that colloquium was wrong. And I think my colloquium was on a Wednesday, on Friday there was a drop of 13 million lines of code from the axioms Fermat's Last Theorem is proved.

How it was done? So, it was Anthropic, and how it was done was AI systems just got really, really good at it. It's still not a nice proof. It's not the clean proof, it's not—there's still a ton of work that needs to be done to make that proof a mathlib quality proof. And I think that's important work and work that we want to do. But in terms of just getting that sorry-free theorem, they just blasted through all of these. They jerry-rigged this. They jerry-rigged that. Steven

顺便说一句，这总是很美妙——因为一切变化太快，会有很多陈述，每个人、世界专家做出的陈述，是错误的。这就是重点。人类讨论的有趣之处就在于我们有分歧，我敢说今天所说的所有内容中，大约 40% 到 80% 在 5 年后会被证明是错的。因为一切变化太快。但这正是人类讨论、审议、话语乃至科学过程的重点：说出来，看看什么能站得住脚。

That's the whole point of it. 那么，黎曼表明的是，嗯，记住我们讨论的是这个函数，它有复数输入，有这些输出，我们关心的是输出何时为零。所以他表明存在所谓的临界带，这是复平面上的一个垂直无限带，实部，你可以认为 X 坐标在 0 和 1 之间，虚部，Y 坐标，可以任意高或任意低。而猜想是零点都位于那个带的正中央。实部为二分之一。这正是 Ankur 刚才说的。而直到周二我们所知道的是 zeta 函数没有零点的区域。这个区域随着你往上走会越来越窄。它趋向于那条线。那条线是所谓素数定理，它告诉你素数的基本分布等价于这个函数在实部 S 等于 1 的那条线上没有零点。所以，如果 X 坐标是 1，当你沿着那条线上下走，你永远不会找到应用 zeta 函数后它为零的值。明白吗？所以，我们有一个无零区域，随着你往上走越来越窄。这给了我们已知最好的素数分布，素数定理中最好的误差项。如果你告诉我你想讨论多少数学，我可以深入讲。

And so, what Riemann showed is that, uh, remember we're talking about this function that has complex inputs and has, uh, these outputs and we're worried about when the output is zero. So, he showed that there's what's called the critical strip, which is, uh, vertical infinite strip in the complex plane where the real part is, so you can think about the X coordinate lies between zero and one, and the imaginary part, the Y coordinate, can go arbitrarily high or arbitrarily low. And the hypothesis is that the zeros all lie right in the dead center of that strip. Where the real part is a half. That's exactly what Ankur was just saying. And what we knew until Tuesday was region where there are no zeros of the zeta function. That's like getting thinner and thinner the higher up you go. It goes towards the one line. The The one line is something called the prime number theorem, which tells you the basic distribution of the prime numbers is equivalent to the fact that there are no zeros of this function on the line, uh, real part of S equals one. So, if the X coordinate is one, as you go up and down that line, you will never find, uh, value where when you apply the zeta function, it it hits zero. Okay? So, we had the zero-free region that was a that was getting thinner and thinner the higher up you go. And that gave us the best known distribution on the prime numbers, the best error term in the prime number theorem. Which I can go into if you You tell me how much math you want to You want to talk about.

纳维-斯托克斯方程 Navier-Stokes Equations

Host

说到这个，Steve，你给我们讲过一个关于汤的故事，可能让你爱上了流体动力学这个更大的问题。所以，从数学到应用数学再到工程，整个链条。也许你能谈谈纳维-斯托克斯方程，这套解释流体的方程，为什么它如此重要，为什么它美丽。就像我们谈论素数一样，为什么它如此困难，以及纳维-斯托克斯被证明为什么是件大事。

So speaking of which, Steve, you told us a story about soup that made you maybe fall in love with the bigger problem of fluid dynamics. So, going from math to applied math to engineering, the full thing. So maybe can you talk about Navier-Stokes, these set of equations that explain fluids, why it's such a big deal, why it's beautiful. Similar as we talked about primes, why is it so hard and why is it such a big deal that Navier-Stokes was proven.

Steven

从根本上说，纳维-斯托克斯方程只是陈述流体动量守恒的一种方式，适用于气体和液体这类可以相对自由运动的物体。我们知道 F=MA 是动量守恒，经典牛顿物理学。同样的基本原理适用于这个房间里的气体、流动的水，并且在任何体积中都成立。所以它在小体积、大体积、假想体积中都成立。如果你在一个体积中写下动量守恒，然后取该体积任意小的极限，就得到这些纳维-斯托克斯方程。它是一个微分方程，一个偏微分方程，描述流体的速度场如何在空间和时间中变化，以及与该流体所受作用力相关的压力。

So, fundamentally the Navier-Stokes equations is just a way of stating the conservation of momentum for fluids, for objects that can move kind of freely like gases and liquids. So, we know F equals MA is conservation of momentum, classical Newtonian physics. The same basic principle applies to the gas in this room, to water flowing, and it's true in any volume. So, it's true in little volumes, big volumes, kind of imaginary volumes. And if you write down that conservation of momentum in a volume, and then you take the limit as that volume becomes arbitrarily small, you get these Navier-Stokes equations. It's a differential equation, a partial differential equation, that describes how the velocity field of a fluid changes in space and time, and also the pressure related to the forces acting on that fluid.

Host

你能直观解释一下吗？既然 F=MA 是个相当简单的方程，为什么流体是这么复杂的系统？

Can you give intuition about why fluid, given that F equals MA is a pretty simple equation, why fluids are such complicated systems?

Steven

F=MA 写起来简单，但它可以变得非常非常快就复杂起来。比如，如果我有单摆，写出单摆的方程很容易。如果我在它末端再挂一个摆，现在方程就更复杂了，而且会产生混沌。流体在某种意义上，如果我要模拟这个房间里的空气，我可以把房间切成十亿个小盒子，为这十亿个小盒子中的动量变化写下微分方程，但现在我得到了一个非常复杂的方程组。所以，它写起来简单，概念上理解方程在说什么、代表什么也很简单，但真正求解它、以任何实际意义理解流体如何运动，是非常非常复杂的。

F equals MA is simple to write down, but it can get complicated really, really quickly. So, if I have like a single pendulum, it's really easy to write down the equations for that single pendulum. If I put another pendulum on the end of it, now I have a more complicated equation, and it gives chaos. Fluids is, in a sense, if I try to model the air in this room, I could chop the room up into a billion little boxes, and write down a little differential equation for how the momentum works in all of those billion little boxes, but now I have a really complicated system of equations. And so, it's simple to write down, it's simple to conceptually understand what the equation is saying and representing, but actually solving it and understanding how the fluid actually moves in any kind of practical sense is very, very complicated.

Steven

好消息是，如果你能提高我们预测湍流的能力，哪怕只是稍微更好、更快、更准确地模拟它，有几十个领域可以通过相对较小的改进产生巨大影响——包括经济、生态、人类健康和安全。所以，减少飞机阻力、更好的人工心脏、更高效的风力涡轮机和燃气轮机，等等。世界是——我们生活和工作在流体中，我们所有的机器也是如此。逆设计问题。我知道它实际上与这类数学问题密切相关。我知道我想证明什么，但我不知道如何到达那里。我知道我想要一个高升力、低阻力的机翼，但我不知道改变几何形状、改变机翼形状会如何影响这一连串越来越小的涡流——在机翼上产生的湍流流体——最终导致其升力和阻力。

The inverse design problem. I know it's actually very closely related to this kind of math problem. I know what I want to prove. I don't know how to get there. I know I want a wing that has high lift and low drag, but I don't know how changing the geometry, changing the shape of that wing, will affect this whole cascade of smaller and smaller and smaller vortices—this turbulent fluid that generates over the wing—that eventually results in its lift and drag.

逆向设计与方程极限 Inverse Design and the Limits of Equations

Ankur

我不知道如何高效地做逆向设计。部分原因是这些方程模拟或测试起来很昂贵。风洞很贵，你需要超级计算机来模拟这些东西。这其实和纯数学有点关系——我想我们待会儿会回到这个话题。但费曼有一句非常好的话：我们知道如何写下这些非常通用的方程——麦克斯韦方程、纳维-斯托克斯方程、薛定谔方程——但我们目前无法从方程本身知道哪些现象是可能的。如果你从未到过地球，你能仅凭一个偏微分方程就预测出日落、潮汐和所有这些现象吗？我们离那还差得远。而且还有一些非常根本的数学问题：如果我能写下一个微分方程，我却无法告诉你它里面包含什么。我们仍然必须模拟，仍然必须测试，仍然必须走这套非常繁复的工程流程，才能理解这些方程如何表现为机翼上的阻力，或人工心脏内部的湍流。

I don't know how to inverse design efficiently. Part of it is because the equations are expensive to simulate or to test. Wind tunnels are expensive. You need supercomputers to simulate these things. This is actually kind of related to pure math—we'll get back to this, I think. But there's this really nice Feynman quote: we know how to write down these very general equations—Maxwell's equations, Navier-Stokes equations, Schrödinger's equations—but we currently don't know from the equation what phenomena are possible. If you never visited Earth, could you have possibly predicted the sunsets and the tides and all the phenomena just from a partial differential equation? We're not even close to there. And there are some very fundamental math questions about—if I can write down a differential equation, I can't tell you what's inside of it. And we still have to simulate, we still have to test, we still have to do this very elaborate engineering process to understand how these equations manifest drag on an airplane wing or turbulence inside of an artificial heart.

抱歉，我能就这一点问个问题吗？我一直想问。比如在爱因斯坦的广义相对论中，他写下了这些方程。几年后，施瓦西发现了一个爆炸（奇点）。大家就说：“哦，得了吧。这些方程到底哪里有问题，才会出现爆炸？如果你把足够多的物质塞进足够小的空间，光就无法逃逸。我们没有任何物理经验支持这个。这一定是方程的错误。”直到我们发现了黑洞。

And then it turns out that actually the theory perfectly explains—it predicts what's supposed to happen. Ankur

是的，这是个很好的问题。我给你一个工程上的回答：这些方程在某个尺度上是对流体如何运作的极佳模型。你说得完全正确——你需要在一个体积内对一定数量的单个分子取平均，这些本构方程才成立。所以如果你放大到足够小的尺度，现在我们谈论的是单个分子，纳维-斯托克斯方程就失效了。所以这些方程爆炸的数学问题从数学角度看很有趣。等到它们爆炸时，它已经不再是流体了。

Yeah, I mean, it's a great question. I'm going to give you the engineering answer, which is these equations are an exceptionally good model of how fluids work at a certain scale. And you're absolutely right—you need some number of individual molecules to be averaged over in a volume for these to be constitutive equations that are valid. And so if you zoom in small enough, now we're talking about individual molecules and Navier-Stokes breaks down. So the mathematical problem of these equations blowing up is interesting from a mathematical standpoint. By the time they blow up, it stops being a fluid.

Ankur

But this idea that we don't know something so fundamental about these equations—there are so many people on Earth right now simulating and testing Navier-Stokes in every industry imaginable. It's probably one of the most studied and analyzed and used equations in the entire engineering world. And we don't know this fundamental property of the PDE: does it blow up in finite time or not? That's like the zeroth-order version of the bigger question: what other kinds of solutions does it admit? Are there sunsets and hurricanes and tornadoes and all the phenomenology that could exist? Existence and uniqueness is like the simplest baby step. And that's where it gets interesting to me: even if there's not a direct tangible use of this mathematical result in an engineering sense, it gets us one step closer to understanding the nature of these equations and what is possible, which is very fundamentally interesting to us.

纳维-斯托克斯方程有哪些不同的组成部分？你能深入讲讲吗？描述流体需要什么？

Ankur

好，非常非常基础：流体在运动。它在不同地点和不同时间以不同方式运动。所以流体的基本构件是速度场。在空间中的每一点都有一个速度矢量，告诉你流体如何运动。也许这里很快，那里很慢，就形成了一个涡旋。

Yeah, so very, very basic: a fluid moves. And it moves differently in different places and at different points in time. And so the basic building block of a fluid is the velocity field. So at every point in space there is a velocity vector that tells you how that fluid is moving. Maybe it's really fast here and slow here, and it creates a vortex.

流体动力学基础 Fluid Dynamics Basics

Steven

当流体速度在空间上变化很大时，事情就变得更有趣了。比如龙卷风就是一个在空间和时间上都变化非常快的流体速度场。其中涉及压力。龙卷风的核心是低压。所以压力在推动这个速度场，使其以某种方式运动。基本构件是速度场和压力。然后方程告诉你速度如何根据自身的动量在时间和空间上变化。流体有动量来携带自身，这就是阵风。它有内部摩擦和阻力，这就是黏度。蜂蜜比水有更大的内部阻力，水比空气有更大的内部阻力。然后所有外力，比如加热、压力和重力等。这些都可以量化为这个更大的纳维-斯托克斯方程中的项。

Um and it gets more interesting when the fluid velocity changes a lot over space. So like a tornado is a very rapidly changing fluid velocity field in both space and time. Um there's pressures that are involved. So again, like the core of a tornado is low pressure. So pressures are um kind of forcing this velocity field and causing it to move in the way it does. That's the building block is a velocity field and its pressures. And then the equations tell you how that velocity changes in time and in space according to kind of its own momentum. So fluid has momentum to carry itself. That's, you know, gusts of wind. It has internal friction and resistance. That's viscosity. So honey has more internal resistance than water, has more internal resistance than air. Um and then all of the external forces like um heating and pressure and gravity and things like that. Um so each of those can be quantified as a term in this this larger Navier-Stokes equation.

Host

说到外力，你能描述一下纳维-斯托克斯方程数学方面的爆炸吗？问题的表述是什么，实际证明了什么，为什么这么难？

So speaking of external forces, can you describe the math side of this of the Navier-Stokes with a blow up? What was the formulation of the problem and what was actually proven and why was it so hard?

Steven

如果没有内部摩擦，即黏度，我们强烈怀疑方程在某些条件下会爆炸。我们有其他例子，更简单的问题类似于纳维-斯托克斯方程，确实会爆炸。最简单的就是一个简单的常微分方程，如果你不介意我稍微跑题一下。

If you don't have that internal friction, that viscosity, we have a strong suspicion that the equations can and will blow up under certain uh conditions. We have other examples, kind of simpler problems that are like Navier-Stokes, uh that do blow up. So the simplest one is just a simple ordinary differential equation, if you don't mind me taking a little tangent.

Steven

所以 x. = x²。x 是系统的状态。x. 表示 x 随时间的变化率。如果 x 的变化率与 x² 成正比增长，那个微分方程确实会在有限时间内爆炸。所以只需要一个二次非线性项。想想如果我有 x. = x。这就像 x 可以是兔子的数量。我们知道兔子喜欢繁殖。x. = x 呈指数增长。兔子数量随时间呈指数增长，会变得越来越大，翻倍再翻倍。但它不会在有限时间内达到无穷大，那只是正常的指数增长，不是有限时间爆炸。那是 x. = x。但如果我取 x. = x²，那就会在有限时间内爆炸。所以二次非线性，这种二次反馈足以导致方程爆炸。如果你写下这个，你可以用经典的基本微积分技巧求解，你会得到有限时间爆炸。所以有一系列偏微分方程具有相同的行为，其中二次非线性项。有趣的是，纳维-斯托克斯方程中描述流体如何凭借自身动量运动的对流项就是一个二次非线性项，就像 x. = x² 一样。这是自运动。没有扩散，没有黏度，我们认为那可能会在有限时间内爆炸到无穷大。但我们不知道黏度，即像蜂蜜那样的内部摩擦和阻力，是否总是能在爆炸发生前将其阻尼掉。作为工程师，我的直觉是当然会。如果有一个加速到无穷大的流动，它应该立即扩散到邻近区域，产生扩散并抑制增长。所以我认为数学问题本质上是：是否存在任何边缘情况，任何流体的初始配置，使得你能完美地编排，使其在有限时间内加速到无穷大。然后有两个技术版本：一个没有外部强迫，一个有光滑外部强迫。光滑很重要，因为如果外部强迫有奇点，那就变得平凡了。比如，如果我用龙卷风强迫，一个无限紧的龙卷风，那当然会爆炸。

So x. = x². x is the state of my system. x. just means the rate of change of x in time. And if that rate of change of x is is growing proportional to x² that differential equation does blow up in finite time. So it just takes a a quadratic nonlinearity. Um think about if I have, you know, x. = x. This is like x could be the population of bunnies. And we know bunnies like to populate. x. = x grows exponentially. You know, the the state of my bunny population in time will grow exponentially. It'll get bigger and bigger and bigger. It'll double and double and double. But it won't reach infinity until infinite time is past. That's just a normal exponential growth. That's not finite time blow up. That's x. = x. But if I take x. = x², that blows up in finite time. So that quadratic nonlinearity, that quadratic kind of feedback is enough to cause this equation to blow up. And you can like if if you write this down, um you know, you can solve this using kind of classic basic, uh, calculus techniques and you get this this this finite time blow up. Um, and so there's a progression of partial differential equations that have the same behavior where a quadratic non-linearity and that's really interesting is the Navier-Stokes equations one of the terms that can the convective term that tells how the fluid kind of moves itself with its own momentum is a quadratic non-linearity just like x. = x squared. It's kind of this self-motion. Um, without diffusion, without viscosity we think that that should probably blow up, uh, to infinity in finite time, have this finite time blow up. But we don't know if viscosity, if the kind of, you know, internal friction and resistance like honey has always damps out those blow ups before they can happen. My intuition as an engineer is, of course they do. Like if I have a a flow where it's you know, accelerating, uh, you know, to infinity, that should immediately diffuse into its neighbors. It should create this like diffusion into its its neighboring fluid and kind of tamp down that that growth. And so I think the math problem is essentially are there any edge cases, any initial configurations of the fluid where you perfectly orchestrate this thing so that it could actually accelerate to infinity in finite time. Uh, and then there's two kind of technical versions of this. One is with no external forcing and one is with smooth external forcing. Smooth being important because if the external forcing has singularities, then it's kind of trivial. Um, like if I force it with a tornado, you know, an infinitely tight tornado, then of course it's going to blow up.

是的，三维湍流中的一个有趣现象是，通常大涡旋分解成小涡旋，再分解成更小的涡旋，直到最终分子扩散成热。这就是三维湍流级联。在二维中，比如地球上的天气，实际上会出现反向级联，这就是飓风形成的原因。小涡旋合并成中等涡旋，再合并成更大的涡旋。所以 AI 证明的这种反例，即可能存在有限时间爆炸，基本上创建了一个非常特殊的涡旋，在第三维上拉伸并变薄，使其本质上变得无限薄。半径随着拉伸越来越小。就像花样滑冰运动员旋转时，把手臂收进来，转得快得多。这种涡旋的收缩速率非常特殊，使得流体中的能量保持有限，但在局部丝状区域，流动爆炸，得到无限涡量。在 AI 证明之前，数学家们开始认为有可能找到这个反例，这让我感到惊讶。

Yeah, so so one of the interesting phenomena in three-dimensional turbulence is you generally have this this breakdown of big vortices into smaller vortices into little itty-bitty vortices until eventually they molecularly diffuse into heat. 湍流是这种现象：当你有一个复杂的流体在翻滚、混合和运动，就像达芬奇那些美丽的画作，描绘了各种不同尺度的流体。所有尺度都重要。所有的大涡、中涡和小涡，它们实际上都对预测升力和阻力等有影响。所以，这个房间里的气体是一个很好的反例。它与湍流相反。它很复杂。分子都以非常疯狂的方式运动。而且有很多分子。但是，你知道，10 的 23 次方个气体粒子，所有那些个体构型都不重要。我们从热力学知道，这是经典物理学的巨大成功，只有少数几个数字真正决定这种气体的行为，比如温度、压力、密度。这种简单的描述对于湍流流体、机翼上的流动、你心脏内的流动、河流中的流动都不存在。所有尺度实际上都重要。模拟中的所有数字实际上都重要，以预测下一个行为，预测会发生什么。这使得它从根本上具有挑战性。所以，这实际上是一个伟大的 AI 问题。是否存在一个坐标系？是否有一组数字，就像我们对气体有温度、压力、密度一样？是否有一组数字或某种湍流流体的表示，使其能够以对工程有用的方式简单描述，以建造电机和发动机，就像我们对气体动力学那样？

复杂系统与生物飞行者 Complex Systems and Biological Flyers

Ankur

所以，我认为这是描述问题的一个很好的方式。我会举两个极端的例子。地球的天气和气候系统实际上只是复杂。所有尺度都很重要。这个邮政编码区域的天气对我很重要。一切都很重要。所有变量都很重要。你说得对。如果我把那个系统过度简化为五个数字，那么我就失去了我真正关心的描述能力。这是一个极端。另一个极端，让我们想想飞蛾或蝴蝶或果蝇是如何飞行的。它们通过存在证明了你可以与极其复杂的湍流相互作用，并进行非常先进的工程控制。它们可以在阵风和雨中控制它们的力和力矩。它们可以过着非常丰富的生活，而它们大概没有流体动力学博士学位，也不知道纳维-斯托克斯方程。它们可能没有运行超级计算机模拟。可能只有五六个数字对它们维持前缘涡和失速翅膀以栖息和着陆的日常生活真正重要。这些是两个极端的例子，我真的希望能够处理这两种情况。我们希望能够处理真正复杂系统的全部复杂性，并且我们希望能够提炼出绝对最低限度的 essentials，以便在那些昆虫极端情况下做出最快的决策。而且生物飞行器在许多情况下仍然大大优于人类设计的流体系统。所以关于这些简单系统，我们还有很多要学习。

So, I think that's a really nice way of framing the problem. And I would take two extreme examples. So, Earth's weather and climate systems are actually just complex. All the scales do matter. The weather in this zip code matters to me. Everything matters. All the variables matter. And you're right. Like if I over reduce that system to, you know, five numbers then I lose the descriptive capability of what I actually care about. That's one extreme. The other extreme, let's think about how a moth flies or a butterfly or a fruit fly. They are proof by existence that you can interact with an extremely complicated turbulent flow and do very advanced engineering control. They can control their forces and moments in gusts, in rain. They can live very rich lives and they presumably don't have a PhD in fluid dynamics or know the Navier-Stokes. They're probably not running supercomputer simulations. There might be five or six numbers that actually matter to their daily life of maintaining a leading edge vortex and stalling their wings to perch and to land. And those are kind of two extreme examples and I really want to be able to handle both of those. We want to be able to handle the full complexity of truly complex systems and we want to be able to distill down the absolute bare minimum essentials for fastest decisions in those kind of insect extreme cases. And biological flyers still dramatically outperform human designed fluid systems in many regimes. So we still have a lot to learn about these simple systems.

自然中的鲁棒优化 Robust Optimization in Nature

Steven

我们现在有点转向研究了，但你知道，对我来说，大自然不仅解决非常困难的优化问题，而且似乎找到非常稳健的解决方案，这总是令人惊叹。我总是被这样一个事实所震撼：当我们建模时，我们倾向于假设我们拥有完整的信息，比如纳维-斯托克斯方程的边界和初始条件。但当鸟飞行时，它并不知道所有这些。在某种程度上，用不确定性来建模事物，实际上是你重新构建优化问题以获得非常丰富和稳健解决方案的方式。令我惊讶的是，许多日常例子都符合这一点。我记得，我是一个棒球迷，我读过一篇关于棒球的数学论文，当你有一个外野手时，一旦球被击出，很多东西都是已知的，比如速度、轨迹。你会认为外野手会直接走直线到他们预期球的位置。但当你观察外野手时，他们不会走直线路径，他们会走一条轻微的曲线。那为什么呢？至少一种理解方式是，这实际上是一个非常稳健的策略，因为当你走曲线路径时，你可以不断重新评估球的轨迹，然后根据需要调整。所以大自然找到了非常有趣的解决方案，但首先什么才是优化问题的正确表述并不总是显而易见的。有时在不确定性情况下建模，以便设计出对没有完整系统知识这一事实具有稳健性的解决方案，似乎是重要的一环。

We're like veering into research now, but you know, to me it's always amazing how nature not only solves very hard optimization problems. It seems to find very robust solutions. And I'm always struck by the fact that you know, when we model it, we tend to model it assuming we have full knowledge, you know, about what the boundary and initial conditions are for things like Navier-Stokes. But when a bird flies, you know, it doesn't know all of that. And to some extent, you know, modeling things with uncertainty is really how you reframe the optimization problem to get very, you know, rich and robust solutions. It's kind of amazing to me how a lot of everyday examples sort of fit into this. I remember, you know, I'm a big baseball fan and I remember reading a math paper about baseball that when you've got an outfielder, you know, once the ball has been hit, a lot of stuff is known, you know, the velocity, the trajectory. You would think the outfielder would just take a straight line path to where they expected the ball to be. But when you observe outfielders, they don't take straight line paths, they take a slight curve to it. So why is that? You know, at least one way to wrap your head around it is that that's actually a very robust strategy, cuz when you're taking a curved path, you can continually re-evaluate the trajectory of the ball and then adjust as needed. So there are very interesting solutions that nature finds, but it's not always obvious what is the right formulation for the optimization problem in the first place. And sometimes modeling it in the case of uncertainty so that you can design solutions that are robust to the fact that you don't have full system knowledge seems to be an important piece of the picture.

昆虫飞行与部分信息 Insect Flight and Partial Information

Steven

所以有一个有趣的题外话与此相关。昆虫特别没有能力测量其完整的流场。它们能对阵风做出反应，阵风的速度比信息从翅膀传到大脑再传回肌肉的速度还要快。所以研究人员在生物学中研究这个问题已经几十年了。他们发现翅膀上嵌入了应变敏感神经元。可能有几十到几百个，位于非常固定的位置。所以，兄弟果蝇会有相同位置的这些应变敏感神经元。当翅膀以特定的时间模式弯曲时，这些应变敏感神经元就会放电。所以，当翅膀被阵风吹到，翅膀弯曲，神经元放电，昆虫肩部肌肉中发生局部计算，直接基于部分信息进行稳健的校正，而这个系统它没有方程，是非线性和湍流的。所以，这就像再次证明存在性，正是你所说的，也许稳健的策略就是真正依赖部分信息和不确定性。它们用少得多的信息就能做到比我们现在更多的事情。

So there's a fun aside related to this. Insects in particular don't have the ability to measure their full flow field. They can react to gusts of wind, gusts of wind faster than the information that hits their wings goes to their brain and back to their muscles. So researchers have studied this for decades in biology. And what they found is that there are strain-sensitive neurons embedded on the wings. Maybe a few dozen to a few hundred of these in very stereotypical locations. So, brother fruit flies will have the same locations of these strain-sensitive neurons. And those strain-sensitive neurons fire when the wing bends in a certain temporal pattern. So, it gets hit by a gust of wind, the wing bends, neurons fire, and a local computation is happening in that shoulder muscle of this insect that goes directly back and corrects robustly based on partial information of a system it doesn't have equations for that is non-linear and turbulent. So, it's like again, proof by existence is exactly what you're saying is maybe the robust strategy of actually relying on partial information and uncertainty. They can do more than we can at this point with way less information.

黑洞与曲速引擎 Black Holes and Warp Drives
