唯一游戏猜想 Unique Games Conjecture

Host

Cory，我最喜欢的话题之一是理论计算机科学。你是许多问题的世界专家。所以，我想问你一个在 OpenAI 工作中解决的大问题，即唯一游戏猜想。你能给人们一个直觉，这个问题的本质是什么？它是什么——有什么大不了的？证明中有哪些有趣的方面？

And Cory, one of my favorite topics is theoretical computer science. You're one of the world experts in many of its problems. So, I'd love to ask you about one of the big problems that's been solved in this OpenAI job, which is the unique games conjecture. Can you give people an intuition about what this problem is? What is it—what is the big deal? And what are some aspects of the proof that are interesting?

Steven

是的，我很乐意。所以，让我给一点背景。让我们从复杂性理论 101 开始。理论计算机科学中最重要的一个问题就是可满足性问题。所以，你要记住的是，想象你有变量 X1 到 Xn，它们可以取真或假的值。我要做的是用它们创建一个大的公式。通常我们这样做是用或的与。所以我从一个子句开始。那就是，你知道，一个或，X1 或非 X3 或 X7。所以，根据我的赋值，那个子句可能被满足，只要其中至少一个为真。然后我把许多这样的不同子句用与连接起来。问题是，如果我给你那个公式，称为合取范式公式，你能找到一个可满足的赋值吗？所以，你能找到一个 X1、X2 直到 Xn 的赋值，将它们设置为真或假，使得所有那些或子句都为真，从而使整个公式评估为真？所以，这是一个典型例子：容易检查别人的答案，但自己找到答案可能很难。所以，如果你告诉我它是可满足的，我要做的就是问你，‘好的，给我可满足的赋值。’你告诉我 X1 设置为什么，X2 设置为什么，我就逐个子句检查，确保你真的满足了所有。现在，理论计算机科学的核心问题是 P 与 NP 问题。所以，容易检查的问题，是否容易找到它们的解？我们领域的假设是不，不容易。这是许多事物的基础，比如密码学和电子商务等等。现在，真正通向唯一游戏猜想的起点是所谓的 PCP 定理。这是 90 年代证明的一个里程碑式的结果。它实际上如此重要，以至于登上了《纽约时报》的头版。它的关键思想是，即使稳健地欺骗验证者也是困难的。所以，你从一个公式开始，它要么可满足，要么不可满足，对吧？那是什么意思？如果可满足，你可以写下一个赋值，我可以逐一检查整个东西评估为真。但如果不可满足，那意味着某个地方你在撒谎。你写下这个声称的可满足赋值，当我逐个子句检查时，我很快会发现一个子句不真，这使整个无效。我说，‘啊哈，我抓到你了。它实际上不是可满足的赋值。’所以，概率可检查证明等背后的思想，不深入技术定义，其实就是间隙放大。所以，你能从一个难以决定是否可满足的公式开始吗？你能设计一个新的、可能大得多的公式，它稳健地容易检查吗？在某种意义上，如果可满足，它仍然可满足。你可以写下一个赋值，没问题。但如果不可满足，我应该能够只是抛几个硬币，查询随机变量，检查一个随机子句，并且有很大概率发现你在撒谎。所以，关键是，通过非常次线性的检查，只用常数量的检查，我就能发现你在作弊。所以，这是理论计算机科学中的一个重大成果，因为它真正为所谓的近似困难性奠定了基础。复杂性理论中的许多算法是，当你面对真正难以解决的问题时你做什么？你知道，我们做的一件事是尝试近似解决它们。所以，也许我们找不到一个赋值，如果存在一个满足所有子句的赋值，但我们能找到一个满足许多子句的赋值吗？多大比例的子句？现在，即使在那里，我们也会预期问题在某个点之后变得计算困难。

Yes, I'd love to. So, let me give you a little bit of a background. So, let's start off with complexity theory 101. So, one of the most important problems in theoretical computer science is the satisfiability problem. So, the thing to keep in your mind is imagine you've got variables X1 to Xn, and they can take on either true or false values. And what I'm going to do is I'm going to create a big formula out of them. And usually the way we do this is with an AND of ORs. So, I start off with the clause. That's, you know, an OR, X1 or not X3 or X7. So, depending on my assignment, that clause might be satisfied as long as at least one of those things is true. And then I take the AND of many of these different clauses together. And the question is, if I give you that formula, called a conjunctive normal form formula, you know, can you find a satisfying assignment? So, can you find an assignment of X1, X2 up to Xn, setting them either to be equal to true or false, so that all of those OR clauses are on, so that the entire formula evaluates to true? So, this is the canonical example of a problem that's easy to check someone else's answer, but it might be hard to find an answer yourself. So, if you told me that it's satisfiable, all I would do is I'd ask you, 'Okay, give me the satisfying assignment.' You tell me what X1 is set to, what X2 is set to, and I just go clause by clause and check to make sure that you really have satisfied everything. Now, the central problem in theoretical computer science is the P versus NP problem. So, are problems that are easy to check, is it easy to find their solutions to? And the presumption in our field is that no, it's not easy. And that's the basis of a lot of things like cryptography and e-commerce and what have you. Now, really the starting point for getting to something like the unique games conjecture is something called the PCP theorem. So, this was a landmark result proved in the '90s. It actually was such a big deal that it made the front page of the New York Times. And the crux of it is really this idea that it's even robustly hard to fool a verifier. So, you start off with a formula, and either it's satisfiable or not satisfiable, right? And what does that mean? If it's satisfiable, you can write down an assignment, and I can check one by one that the entire thing evaluates to true. But if it's not satisfiable, that means some place you're lying. You write down this purported satisfying assignment, and when I go clause by clause, I'm quickly going to find one clause that is not true, and that invalidates the whole thing. And I say, 'Aha, I caught you. It's not actually a satisfying assignment.' So, the idea behind things like probabilistically checkable proofs, without getting into a lot of the technical definitions, the idea is really gap amplification. So, can you start off with a formula that is hard to decide whether it's satisfiable or not? And can you engineer a new, maybe much bigger formula that's robustly easy to check? In the sense that if it's satisfiable, it'll still be satisfiable. You can write down an assignment and it'll be fine. But if it's not, I should actually be able to just flip a few coins, query random variables, check a random clause, and with good probability find that you're lying. So, the point is that with very sublinear inspection, with only constant amount of inspection, I can find that you're cheating. So, this was a momentous result in theoretical computer science because it laid the foundation really for what's called hardness of approximation. So, a lot of algorithms in complexity theory is what do you do when you're faced with problems that are genuinely hard to solve? And, you know, one of the things we do is we try to approximately solve them. So, maybe we can't find an assignment, if there is one that satisfies all the clauses, but can we find one that satisfies many of the clauses? How large a fraction of the clauses? Now, even there we would expect problems to become computation hard after a certain point.

PCP定理与唯一游戏猜想 PCP theorem and the Unique Games Conjecture

Steven

问题不只是判断它是否完全可满足很难，甚至判断它是 100% 可满足还是只有 99% 可满足也可能很难。最好的情况是，是否所有赋值都满足 99% 的子句？这本身可能也很难判定。这就是概率可检验证明给我们的东西。在我们已知优化问题很难的地方，它们告诉我们连近似都很难。而唯一游戏猜想是对普通 PCP 定理的加强，因为它涉及某一类标签覆盖实例。细节有点技术性，不便展开。但关键在于，如果那个版本的概率可检验证明成立，它会有很好的组织效果：对一大片问题，我们实际上会知道什么是最好的近似算法，而且会是非常简单的东西。

It should be that it's not just that deciding whether it's completely satisfiable or not completely satisfiable is hard, but maybe even deciding is it 100% satisfiable or is it only 99% satisfiable? The best case of are all assignments satisfied most 99% of the clauses? That too might end up being hard to decide. So, these are what probabilistically checkable proofs give us. In places where we knew that the optimization problem was hard, they tell us that it's even hard to approximate. And the unique games conjecture is a strengthening of the ordinary PCP theorem because it has to do with a certain type of label cover instance. It's a bit technical to go into the details. But the point is that if that version of a probabilistically checkable proof were true, it would have this great organizing effect. Where it would turn out that for a huge swath of problems, we would actually know what is the best approximation algorithm, and it would be something very simple.

Steven

我刚进入这个领域时，唯一游戏猜想才提出几年。当时关于它应该为真还是为假仍有大量争论。人们努力尝试给出证明，也努力尝试反驳它。大概在 2010 年代有一段很精彩的时期，来回拉锯很多。新结果不断出来，人们对是否相信唯一游戏的看法也随之改变。我喜欢一句俏皮话，我忘了是谁说的，所以没法准确署名，但有人告诉我：也许上帝会根据哪一方更努力来决定它是真还是假。因为它真的似乎一年一变。

So, when I started off in the field, the unique games conjecture had been posed a few years before that. And there was still a lot of debate about whether it should be true or false. People worked hard to try and give proofs of it. People worked hard to try and disprove it. There was this amazing time period maybe in the 2010s where there was quite a lot of back and forth. And new results came out and people's opinions would change about whether they believed unique games or not. I like this quip. I forget who said it, so I can't properly attribute it, but someone told me, maybe God's going to decide whether it's true or false based on which side is working harder. Because it really seemed to change year to year.

Steven

而 OpenAI 那批证明里的一个头条结果是：唯一游戏猜想为真。我得说明，这并非没有人类取得的惊人进展。我在 MIT 的一位同事、在 IAS 的一位同事，和其他人一起贡献了许多重大突破。Dor Minzer 和 Elchanan Dinur 证明了一个较弱的版本，叫做二对一游戏定理。这确实是理论计算机科学界的一记惊雷，因为当时我认为很多人不确定唯一游戏猜想的前进道路在哪里。而我认为他们的工作确实给出了一条可行的前进路径，通向接近唯一游戏的东西。所以听起来，也许人类再花 10 年、20 年就能达到。但当 OpenAI 发布结果时，结果发现唯一游戏猜想实际上是真的。

And one of the headline results in the OpenAI drop of proofs was that the unique games conjecture is true. Now, I should mention that this is not without spectacular human progress on it. One of my colleagues at MIT, one of my colleagues at IAS, they both contributed among other people to a lot of the major strides. So, Dor Minzer and Elchanan Dinur, they proved a weaker version called the two-to-one games theorem. And this really was a shot heard round the world in theoretical computer science because at that point, I think a lot of people weren't sure what was a path forward for the unique games conjecture. And I think their work really gave a viable path forward to something that was in the vicinity of unique games. So, it sounded like maybe humans could get there with another 10, 20 years. But when OpenAI released the results, it turns out that the unique games conjecture is actually true.

Steven

所以这相当了不起，因为有时我确实发现自己站在怀疑者一边，因为唯一游戏听起来太美好，不像是真的。因为很多工作，比如 Prasad Raghavendra 的工作，它意味着对许多不同类型的约束满足问题都有最优的近似难度，这个结论在我看来太强了，不像是真的。

So, this is quite remarkable because at some points I guess I did find myself on the side of the doubters because unique games just sounded too nice to be true. Because a lot of work, like Prasad Raghavendra's work, it implies optimal hardness of approximation for so many different types of constraint satisfaction problems that the conclusion just looked to me too strong to be true.

公告之夜的感想 Reaction on the night of the announcement

Host

那天晚上你得知消息时是什么感受？

What did you feel when you found out that night?

Steven

好，首先，在之前几周就已经有传言说 OpenAI 解决了唯一游戏猜想，而且它是真的。这些甚至不完全是随意的传言。我的一位同事 Dor Minzer 实际上贴了一篇论文，他在底部的脚注里写了类似这样的话：我们本想把这篇论文打磨得更好，但因为流传的传言，我们觉得有必要尽早而不是尽晚地公布我们的方法。所以对我来说，某种意义上结果终于出来了是一种解脱。当然，这些论文不太好读。不是说你指着它就能很快找出新的关键思想，但我很高兴它们出来了，因为现在我们可以进入下一阶段：消化这些证明，弄清楚这个领域之后往哪走。

Okay, well, for starters, there were already rumors in the weeks leading up that OpenAI had solved the unique games conjecture and that it was true. And these weren't even just completely arbitrary rumors. One of my colleagues, Dor Minzer, he actually posted a paper where he wrote something in the footnote at the bottom that we would have liked to polish this paper more, but because of the rumors that were going around, we feel the need to put out our approach sooner rather than later. So, for me, it was in some sense a bit of a relief that the results were finally out there. Now, they're not very readable papers. It's not like you can point to them and really quickly identify what's a new key idea, but I'm glad that they're out there because now we can move forward on the next phase, which is digesting those proofs and figuring out where the field moves after that.

Steven

至于那天晚上我的感受？正如 Alex 提到的，我们手里都拿着饮料，然后我在手机上看到这些证明发布的消息。在晚宴上人们不断过来和我说话，但作为数学家，在数学场合社交本来就很难，更别说你还有真正想读的数学。所以那是难上加难。

Now, in terms of how did I feel that night? As Alex mentioned, we all had a drink in hand and then I saw on my phone the news that these proofs had dropped. And people kept coming up and talking to me at the gala, but it's already hard to socialize at math as a mathematician, let alone when you have math you really want to read. So, it was doubly hard.

Steven

另一件有趣的事是，我当时已经在纽约了，所以我趁晚宴前这个机会去拜访了很多我在 NYU 认识的人。我恰好见到了 Subhash Khot，也就是提出唯一游戏猜想的人。我恰好在晚宴前几个小时、OpenAI 论文发布之前问他：“Subhash，如果他们发布唯一游戏的正向证明，你会怎么做？你会读吗？”他告诉我：“不会。”不幸的是，这是很多人的反应。事实就是，我们在攻克这些宏大挑战，有时当它们被解决时，有时会觉得有人抢了你的风头。

The other funny thing about it was, I was in New York already, so I took it as an opportunity before the gala to visit a lot of people I knew at NYU. And I just happened to be meeting with Subhash Khot, the one who introduced the unique games conjecture. And I just so happened to ask him, just a few hours before the gala, before the OpenAI paper is released, "Subhash, what would you do if they release a positive proof of unique games? Are you going to read it?" And he told me, "No." Which, it's a lot of people's reactions, unfortunately. It's just kind of true that there are these grand challenges we work on, and sometimes when they get solved, sometimes it feels like someone has stolen your thunder.

机器解题时失去了什么 What is lost when machines solve the problem

Steven

并不是说人们没有关于如何解决唯一游戏猜想的想法，我敢打赌，如果给我们更多时间，那些蓝图会成功。我担心的另一点是，我们失去了最初尝试抵达那些解决方案的人类努力。数学不只是关于用我现有的工具能走多远。它是一个凸包吗？是一个子群吗？我不知道。但有时当解决方案是由人驱动的，我们在这个过程中学到的东西要多得多。所以我担心的是，曾经有这些伟大的里程碑问题、定义领域的问题，很多人都在朝它们努力，而决定性的进展出现了，结果一夜之间全部发布，人们失去了沿着那些道路走下去的热情。我想知道，如果我们给那些人空间去走那些路，我们还会发现哪些令人兴奋的东西。

It's not like people didn't have ideas about how to solve the unique games conjecture, and I bet you those blueprints would have panned out if we'd given them more time. I think the other thing I worry is that we lose out on the human effort of trying to reach those solutions to begin with. Math is not just about what is the furthest I can reach with the current set of tools I have. Is it a convex hull? Is it a subgroup? I don't know. But sometimes when it's a human-driven solution, we learn a lot more in the process. So, I worry about the fact that there were these great landmark problems, field-defining problems that a lot of people are reaching towards, and that decisive progress was made, and the results all dropped in one night, and that people lose their enthusiasm for going down those paths, and I wonder if we'd given those people that space to go down those paths, what other exciting things we would have discovered.

在MoMath处理它 Processing it at MoMath

Steven

我想我提到的一件事是，我们当时都在 MoMath 试着消化这件事，有很多非常好的演讲。不是所有都上传到了 YouTube。有些是关于捐助者和以他们命名的奖项。而我不断回到的一个想法是：你最初为什么做数学，是为了玩耍和了解世界。那天晚上我们看到了太多这样的体现。

So, I think one of the things I mentioned, we were all trying to process it at MoMath, and there were a lot of really nice talks. Not all of them went up on YouTube. Some of them were about benefactors and awards named after them. And the thing I kept coming back to is this idea that why do you do math in the first place is to play and learn about the world. And we saw so many manifestations of it that night.

数学玩耍的乐趣 The Joy of Mathematical Play

Ankur

你知道，因为有些奖项是以人名命名的，他们为什么向博物馆捐款？是因为他们希望自己的孩子有一个地方可以玩数学概念、了解世界。那也是埃舍尔展览的开幕式。当我们玩数学概念时会发生什么，这是一个多么好的视觉呈现，对吧？所以我觉得，在这个时刻，当我们共同经历所有这些变化，当我们不知道未来会怎样，不知道 AI 与数学家的合作在未来会是什么样子，不知道招聘会是什么样子时，重要的是要记住，我们所有人进入这个领域的原因是我们非常好奇。我们想理解世界。数学家非常擅长抽象。他们不只是理解一小部分，而是找到一些抽象和一组定义，这些在数学的其他角落会变得异常强大。所以我认为，无论未来如何，确保我们以某种方式组织我们的领域，仍然鼓励人们玩想法，是非常重要的。

You know, because there were awards named after people who, you know, why were they donating to the museum? It was because they wanted their kids to have a place to play with mathematical ideas and learn about the world. It was also the opening of an Escher exhibit. And what a great visual of what happens when we play with mathematical concepts, right? And so it felt to me that in this moment it's really important to remember, as we're going through all of this change together, as we don't know what the future holds, what AI and mathematicians collaboration will look like in the future, what hiring will look like, that the reason we all got into it was because we were very curious. We wanted to understand the world. And mathematicians are very good at abstracting things. They don't just understand one little piece, but they find some abstraction and some set of definitions that turns out to be unreasonably powerful in some other corner of mathematics. So I think it's really important, whatever the future holds, to make sure that we organize our field in a way where we still encourage people to play with ideas.

Ankur

我不知道什么样的奖励制度会奏效，但是你知道，当我观察并与该领域的年轻人交谈时，他们有很多焦虑。他们听到很多故事，甚至是关于他们认识的人，运行工具在网上抓取开放问题，然后尽可能多地灌水论文到 arXiv。我不知道这是不是个好策略。似乎不是，但对我来说肯定不好玩。感觉不像是在玩想法、了解世界。这很可悲。我只想回到我真正享受的事情上。

I don't know what kind of reward system will work, but you know, when I look out and I talk to young people in the field, they have a lot of anxiety. They hear a lot of stories even about people they know, running harnesses to scrape the web for open problems to dump as many papers as they can to flood arXiv. I don't know if that's a good strategy. It doesn't seem like it is, but it certainly doesn't feel like fun to me. It doesn't feel like we're playing with ideas and learning about the world. And that's sad. I just want to get back to the things I really enjoy.

Ankur

也许我还想补充的另一件事是，你知道，对很多人来说这是一场巨大的身份危机。因为你知道，有些人更把自己视为问题解决者，这让人感到非常安慰。你可以在你研究的问题类型、你使用的技术类型、你关心的问题类型周围画一个小圈。这让人感到非常安慰，因为世界其他地方是混乱的，而你戴着眼罩。这是我在乎的部分。现在，我认为是时候摘下那些眼罩了，因为我不觉得那是一种有效的方式。我们喜欢玩，喜欢了解我们周围的世界。重要的是要记住，作为一个年轻的数学家：你是更聪明还是更更好奇？或者你更好奇还是更聪明？最重要的是要更好奇。

The other thing maybe I just want to add in there, too, is like, you know, it's a big identity crisis for a lot of people. Because you know, some people view themselves more as problem solvers and that can feel very comforting. That you can draw a little circle around the types of problems you work on, the types of techniques you use, the types of problems you care about. And that feels very comforting because the rest of the world is messy and you've got blinders on. This is the part that I care about. Now, I think is the time to remove those blinders because I just don't find that to be a productive way to do it. We love playing and learning about the world around us. And it's important to remember as a young mathematician: are you smarter than you are curious or are you more curious than you are smart? It's very important to be more curious than anything else.

Host

你提到了乐趣，那可能是另一个视角。更多关于挣扎。对于我可能是一个外行的视角，一些最有趣的时刻，当然，感觉像是挣扎。我和很多奥运金牌得主交谈过。他们中的许多人在赢得金牌后感到近似抑郁。因为就像你付出了一切，你意识到你付出的一切并没有结束。

Steven

30 年前，有一部关于安德鲁·怀尔斯的纪录片。有一个很棒的场景。我想他们甚至把它放在最开头。对你的金牌得主来说，你知道，一旦你赢得了那个东西，你做什么？他坐在书桌前，描述他意识到解决方案的那一刻。不是他最初的宣布，那是有缺陷的，然后好几个月他试图纠正它，最终他看到了如何做到。他试图说，你知道，我一生中再也不会有像那天在这张书桌前经历的那样强大的时刻了。他哽咽了。他说不出话来。所以，如果你按下按钮，它给了你解决方案，你能得到那种感觉吗？绝对不能。所以，正是对你们两人的观点，就像旅程，你知道，如果你看到一座巨大的山，你说：“总有一天我会登上顶峰。”你开始为此训练，你一点一点地做，不管怎样。然后一架直升机降落，直接把你放在山顶。

Host

你能具体谈谈吗？关于安德鲁·怀尔斯的有趣之处，以及稍微谈谈格里戈里·佩雷尔曼，是长时间独自工作。你怎么看？这在数学中有多常见？坐在那里磨一个问题，在他情况下是 7 年。

Can you actually speak to that? The interesting thing about Andrew Wiles and a little bit about Grigori Perelman as well is working alone for a prolonged period of time. What do you make of that? How common is that in mathematics? Of sitting there and grinding a problem for in his case 7 years.

Alex

直到 20 世纪，几乎每一篇写过的数学论文都是一个人写的。我想现在数学的中位数是两人。所以，我们不是实验室科学。这确实是一个孤独的过程。现在，看，我给我的研究生做的事情不是我在做的事情。算是设计好的。我想让他们有自己的领域。我想为他们打开门。不同领域情况不同，对吧？所以，当他们取得成功时，那真的应该是他们的成功，即使我有想法认为这会有进展，而他们执行了。那是他们的。但就坐在那里而言，我一个月前发表的论文是我思考了 10 年的东西。这是一篇五个作者的论文。在那 10 年里，我们取得了渐进式的进展，直到我们有了解决方案。那篇论文是一个实例：这里有一个现象，我不明白它是什么。这不是“这里有一个问题，解决它”。就像我们在项目开始时无法给你那个需要 sorry 的 Lean 形式化陈述。就像你通过一点一点地攻克问题来发现陈述是什么，直到你看到足够多，说这就是它如何组合在一起的。

Until the 20th century, almost every math paper that had ever been written was written and you know, had one author. I think now the median is two in mathematics. And so, we're not a lab science. It really is a solitary process. Now, see, the things that I give my graduate students to do are not the things that I'm working on. Sort of by design. I want them to have their own field. I want to open doors for them. It's different in different fields, right? So, when they have a success, it really should be their success even if I sort of had an idea that this would go somewhere and they executed on it. That's theirs. But in terms of sitting there for the paper that I put out a month ago is something I've been thinking about for 10 years. It's a five-author paper. Over those 10 years, we made incremental progress to the point where we had a solution. And that paper was an instance of there's a phenomenon here and I don't understand what it is. It wasn't here's a problem solve it. Like we could not at the start of the project give you the lean formalized statement that needs a sorry. Like you discover what the statement is through the process of hacking away at the problem little by little until you see enough of it to say this is how it comes together.

Alex

当然。我认为我在某种程度上通过世界模型思考很多。所以，你知道，我对某些子领域的结果如何发展有一些心理模型。我认为这也很大程度上影响了我如何看待周边领域。所以，对我来说，我的很多工作实际上是在算法思维和其他通常不通过同样算法视角研究的问题之间建立桥梁。所以，我想当我刚开始作为导师时，我犯了一个错误，我给学生的问题 100% 在我的领域。然后问题是，你知道，当他们回来并且有了一个想法时，我可以很快地否决它。我要么对要么错，但无论哪种方式，没有人玩得开心。

Sure. I think I think a lot through world models in some way. So, you know, I have some mental model of how I think results in some sub area go. And I think that colors a lot of how I see surrounding areas, too. So, for me a lot of my work is really about building bridges between algorithmic thinking and other problems that usually aren't studied through that same algorithmic lens. So, I think when I first started as an advisor, I sort of made the mistake that I gave students questions that were 100% in my area. And the problem then was, you know, when they would come back and they'd have an idea, I could shoot it down very quickly. And I was either right or I was wrong, but either way no one was having any fun.

指导与研究方式 Mentoring and Research Approach

Ankur

随着时间的推移，我开始做的是给学生一些与我自己的领域偏离 10 到 20 度的问题。那些我自己想更好地理解的东西，那些我认为重要但一直没时间坐下来读的论文。然后我让学生向我解释结果。我们一起经历这个过程很有趣，不是把论文打印出来逐行阅读，而是试图逆向工程，为什么我们认为这首先能行得通？有很多细节，一旦你掌握了感觉，你就会觉得它们应该能行得通。但至少在重要的论文中，有一个或甚至更多关键想法真正改变了你的视角。哦，这真的可能可行。所以我发现，一旦我们开始尝试理解这些结果之一，我们就会找到自己看待事物的方式。然后不久之后，我们就会发现一些其他问题，我们现在认为这些问题是可以解决的，因为我们找到了自己组织思想的方式。所以，这通常是我的做法：越早让学生处于主导地位越好，不一定有一个明确的问题，而是让他们教我一些东西，让他们负责提炼、消化并抽象出本质。和他们一起用类比推理，这些类比不一定字面真实，但能帮助我们在邻近领域预测什么。然后感觉他们带来了独特的视角，我们就开始行动了。所以，对我来说，在很多方面，技术只会促成这种工作，因为这意味着更多的问题集变得可接近。对我来说，当我找到相关领域的专家，他们真的能用我的语言解释事情时，我会紧紧抓住他们，因为他们能传道。他们能告诉我这个领域是如何组织的，并用我的语言解释。但现在，我也有技术可以做到这一点。而且，失去曾经存在的人文元素也让人感到有点悲伤。

And, you know, over time what I started to do was I would give students questions that were 10-20 degrees off from my own area. Things that I wanted to understand better myself, papers that I thought were important that I just hadn't had time to sit down and read. And then I have the student explain the result to me. And it was fun as we would go through that process together, not by printing out the paper and going through it line by line, but trying to reverse engineer, why do we think this works in the first place? And there's so many details that, once you get the feel for it, you kind of have the sense that they ought to work out. But at least in important papers, there are one or maybe even more than that key ideas that really change your perspective. Oh, this really might be doable. So, what I found was, as soon as we start trying to understand one of these results, we find our own way of looking at things. And then it's not long after we find some other questions that we now think are approachable because we found our own way of organizing our thoughts. So, that's often the way I do it: the earlier that I can have the student in the driver's seat, not necessarily with a well-defined question, but with them teaching me something and them being responsible for distilling and digesting and abstracting it down to its essence. Reasoning through with them analogies that aren't meant to literally be true, but help us figure out what to expect in neighboring areas. Then it feels like they bring a unique perspective and we're off to the races. So, for me in a lot of ways, the technology is only going to enable that kind of work because it means that a much richer set of problems are approachable. For me, when I find experts in related areas that can really explain things in my own language, I hold on to them for dear life because they can speak the gospel. They can tell me how this area is organized and explain it in my own language. But now I have technology that can do that, too. And it also feels a bit sad that you lose that human element that was there once before.

Ankur

我还认为学生们对发表论文的前景非常焦虑，所以最难的部分之一是，当我在思考一个项目时，我真的是在考虑一个多年的时间跨度。因为我有一些想要实现的元愿景，比如，我们如何在量子多体系统中使用学习理论技术？通往这个目标有很多垫脚石，但一些最令人兴奋的问题需要很多年才能解决。但学生们太担心即将到来的人工智能浪潮、工作的未来、普遍基本收入、我们中是否还有人会有工作，所以他们真的在考虑三个月和六个月的期限。然后，就很难找到我们都关心的问题，因为我们思考的是不同的世界。

I think also students have so much anxiety about what publications are going to look like that one of the hardest parts is, when I'm thinking about a project, I'm really trying to think about it in a sort of multi-year time horizon. Because I have some meta vision that I want to realize, like, how can we use learning theory techniques in quantum many-body systems? And there are a lot of stepping stones on route to that, but some of the most exciting problems are many years out. But students are so worried about the incoming AI tidal wave, the future of work, universal basic income, are any of us going to have jobs, that they're really thinking in three- and six-month horizons. And then it's hard to find problems that we both care about just because we're thinking about different worlds.

Host

你不认为我们应该鼓励他们继续思考长期问题吗？我的意思是，我理解这一点

You don't think we should encourage them to continue to think long-term? I mean, I understand that

Ankur

哦，我确实这么认为，但他们并不总是听你的。我的意思是，我不知道我是对是错，也许人工智能会解决我们所有的问题，但似乎此时此刻真正值得我们努力的唯一问题，是那些多年后我们仍然会关心的问题。你应该只研究面向未来的问题。如果你正在研究的事情让你感到匆忙，因为你认为下一代模型肯定会解决那些问题，那么也许就去度假吧，不要解决那个问题。

Oh, I do, but they don't always listen to you. I mean, I don't know if I'm right or wrong, maybe AI will solve all of our problems, but it seems like the only ones that are actually worth us working on at this moment in time are the ones we're still going to care about in a bunch of years. And you should only be working on future-proof problems. If you're working on things that, you're in a rush because you think the next generation of models will definitely solve those problems, then maybe just go on vacation and don't solve that problem.

平衡专注与好奇 Balancing Focus and Curiosity

Host

你能谈谈你如何看待处理许多自然系统吗？可以从这些自然系统中提取出哪些有趣的见解？

Can you speak to how you think you deal with a lot of natural systems? What are the interesting insights that can be pulled out of these natural systems?

Steven

我发现，在专注于解决一个工程问题（这可能非常单一，你会戴上眼罩，只思考这个问题）和给自己精神自由去环顾四周、观察和好奇之间，存在着一种张力。很多时候，当你翻开一块石头时，你会找到解决方案，而这不是你全神贯注思考的事情，而是你思维边缘的事情。如果你通过 peripheral vision 看一个问题，有时你解决它的机会要大得多。所以这对我来说是一个持续的平衡：观察所有我不工作的领域、所有我不理解的自然现象以及所有我好奇的事物，但同时也要有非常专注的问题，你不放松、不放手。我想这对我们所有人都一样。我听到你们两位都提到过多年来一直专注研究的问题，你们没有放手。但想必那是通过有趣的对话和有趣的信息中偶然获得的无数其他信息所启发的。

So, I find a tension between focusing very hard on an engineering problem I'm trying to solve, which can be very singular, you put on blinders, you only think about this problem, and then giving yourself the kind of mental freedom to look around and just observe and be curious. Very often you find the solution when you turn over a rock and when it's not something you're thinking about with the full front of your mind, but in the side of your mind. If you're looking at a problem through your peripheral vision, you have a much better chance of solving it sometimes. And so this is a constant balance for me: observing all of the fields that I don't work in and all of the nature that I don't understand and all of the things that I'm curious about, but then also having very focused problems that you don't relent, you don't let go. And I think this must be true for all of us. I've heard from both of you about focused problems you've been working on for years and decades that you haven't let go of. But presumably that is informed by a million other pieces of information that you happen to pick up through interesting conversations and interesting

邻近领域与例子 Neighboring Fields and Examples

Host

有例子吗？因为听起来邻近领域是一个共同主题。你有没有这样的例子：你大脑中好奇的 peripheral vision 说：“好吧，这是一个有趣的系统。可能相关。”然后最终确实如此，或者未来可能会。

Have example cuz it sounds like neighboring areas is a common theme. Do you have examples of things you're like you're the curious peripheral vision of your brain went, "Okay, that's an interesting system. Might be relevant." And that ended up being so or maybe in the future will.

Steven

所以对我来说，作为一名流体动力学家，生物学的混乱和复杂性，以及材料科学的混乱和复杂性，在某种程度上是相邻的，而且非常富有成果。我从这两个领域学到了很多，我们为处理复杂性而开发的许多基础数学似乎确实可以迁移，有时你有一个想法只是因为你看到某事是可能的，或者你开始开发某物，你可以解释我们以前不理解的东西，但我认为对我来说，生物学、流体和材料具有这种非常有趣的共性：多尺度、非线性、混乱、模糊的目标等等。

So for me as a fluid dynamicist biology and its messiness and complexity and also material science and its messiness and complexity are neighboring in a way that's very fruitful. That's one issue with calculus. The other issue with calculus is the first time you deal seriously with infinity. You have to add up infinitely many infinitely small things and get a finite number and have that number make sense. Um human beings Newton and Leibniz did not understand infinity the way we understand it today. They had an intuition for what it's supposed to represent.

数学史上的危机 Historical Crises in Mathematics

Host

呃，但如果你读牛顿，我的意思是，牛顿在他那个时代因为他对“消逝量的鬼魂”的写法而被嘲笑。你知道，你必须除以零并让它有意义，而你不是在零的时候做，而是在它们变成零之后做。我的意思是，他试图写这些东西的方式导致了 19 世纪的危机，当时柯西和傅里叶证明了相互矛盾的定理。柯西说，你有一个连续函数序列，你取连续函数的极限，你会得到一个连续函数，对吧？极限是一个连续过程，连续函数是连续的，答案必须是连续的。而傅里叶说，我要把一堆正弦和余弦加起来——没有比正弦和余弦更连续的了——我会得到一个锯齿波。我会得到任何你想要的。我会在极限中得到一个不连续函数。他们打破了数学。

Uh but if you read Newton, I mean Newton in his time was ridiculed for the way he wrote about ghosts of departed quantities. I mean, the way that he was trying to write about these things turned into the crisis in the 19th century when Cauchy and Fourier proved conflicting theorems. Cauchy said you have a sequence of continuous functions and you take the limit of continuous functions, you're going to get a continuous function, right? Limits are a continuous process, continuous functions are continuous, the answer has to be continuous. And Fourier says I'm going to add up a bunch of sines and cosines—doesn't get more continuous than sines and cosines—and I'll get a sawtooth. I'll get whatever you want. I'll get a discontinuous function in the limit. They broke math.

Ankur

你知道，我之前在描述 SAT 问题，你想解决的算法问题是，给定任何公式，你想决定它是否可满足。

you know, I was describing the SAT problem earlier, and the algorithmic problem you want to solve is given any formula, you want to decide is it satisfiable or not.

Host

是的。

Yeah.

Ankur

现在，关键的是，当我被要求解决那个算法问题时，我没有告诉你更多关于公式结构的信息。我没有告诉你，你知道，这些是子句的大小，它们看起来像这样。如果我画一个连接它们的图，它会具有这种拓扑结构。所以，这就是最坏情况分析的意义——它基于这样一个前提：你可以通过忽略个体结构的大量细粒度细节来处理所有这些计算问题。你可以尝试解决它们，你知道，对于所有可能的输入。这非常强大，因为我们已经确定了许多算法问题，它们确实可以在最坏情况下解决，并且可以非常高效地解决。

Now, what's crucial there is that when I'm asked to solve that algorithmic problem, I'm not telling you anything more about the structure of the formula. I'm not telling you, you know, these are the size of the clauses, they look like this. If I drew a graph connecting them, it would have this topology. So, that's what worst-case analysis is about—it's about the premise that you can treat all of these computational problems actually by ignoring a lot of the fine-grained detail about the individual structure. And you can try and solve them, you know, for all possible inputs. And that's been spectacularly powerful because we've identified lots of algorithmic problems that really can be solved in the worst case and can be solved really efficiently.

嗯。

Mhm.

Ankur

You know, like finding the max flow uh as an example. But, um the question is, you know, whether or not that's a useful abstraction. So, sometimes maybe you really do need to know more about the structure of the instance faced with in order to solve it better. Right? And that's really an open question. This is the area of what's called beyond worst-case analysis. And I would sort of argue that, you know, it's a very important direction for theoretical computer science to go in. It's hard to figure out what you should articulate about natural problems that is really the right level of detail that your algorithm can exploit it and can do better things than it can in the worst case. 在线算法与不确定性 Online Algorithms and Uncertainty

Ankur

你的目标是，当一个人出现时，你知道他相对于你之前见过的所有人的排名，但你不知道他相对于未来会出现的人的排名。你的目标是当这个人出现时做出决定：你是选择他还是让他过去？以这样一种方式，你有很大机会找到绝对最佳的候选人。所以关键在于，你实际上可以得到一个常数竞争比的算法。你该怎么做：最佳策略是等待一段时间，不做任何决定，不接受任何人，然后一旦这个试用期过去，你就选择第一个比之前见过的任何人都好的人。这将给你一个良好的竞争比，从你可以证明定理的意义上说：假设输入以均匀随机顺序到达，与如果你事先知道输入序列相比，你可能会做得相当好。所以，在在线算法中，存在这种不确定性面纱。那就是：我不知道输入序列会是什么样子。我想无论序列是什么都能做好，因为我真的不知道如何建模那个序列的样子。

And your goal is, when someone comes along, you know their ranking relative to everyone else you've seen before, but you don't know their ranking relative to the people who are yet to come. And your goal is to make a decision when the person comes along: do you commit or do they move on? In such a way that you have a good chance of finding the absolute best candidate. And so the point is that you can actually get a constant competitive ratio algorithm. What you do: the best strategy is to wait some amount of time, not make any decisions, don't accept anyone, and then once that probationary window has passed, you take the first person who's better than anyone you've seen thus far. And that'll give you a good competitive ratio in the sense that you can prove theorems: assuming the inputs arrive in a uniformly random order, you're likely to do pretty well compared to as if you knew what that sequence of inputs was in advance. So there's, under online algorithms, there's this veil of uncertainty. Which is: I don't know what the sequence of inputs are going to look like. I want to do well no matter what that sequence is, because I don't really know how to model what that sequence looks like.

Ankur

现在，你可以换个说法：“嘿，也许我推导出的算法对我的应用来说不够好。我能否通过尝试更多地建模周围的世界来做得更好？”然后，弄清楚你应该建模什么、应该将什么构建到你的模型中、以及不应该构建什么，这有点像一门艺术。在我看来，这似乎是一个极其重要的探索领域。例如，在线算法的另一个非常重要的变体是分页问题。你的笔记本电脑一直在解决这个问题：它收到一系列想要从内存中调取的请求。问题是它实际应该把什么放入缓存，即最容易访问的部分？问题再次在于，你事先不知道那个请求序列是什么样子。所以，你可以研究在线算法问题：什么是管理缓存的好策略？当某些东西被访问时，我必须把它放进去，但我该驱逐什么？你可以设计在竞争比方面表现良好的算法。它们在那些访问中未命中的次数，与如果有人事先告诉我那个请求序列并且我可以仅针对那个序列进行优化的情况下的最佳离线最优解相比，并不会差太多。

Now, you can instead say, "Hey, maybe the algorithms I derive aren't good enough for my applications. Can I do better by trying to model more of the world around me?" And then there's kind of an art to figuring out what you should model, what you should build into your model, and what you shouldn't. And that seems to me like a tremendously important area to explore. So for example, one other variant of online algorithms that's really important is the paging problem. So your laptop solves this problem all the time: it's getting a sequence of requests of things that it wants to recall from memory. And the question is what should it actually put in its cache, the part that's the easiest to access? And the problem again is that you don't know what that sequence of requests looks like in advance. So, you can study the online algorithms problem of: what is a good policy for managing the cache? When something gets accessed, I have to put it in, but what do I evict? And you can design algorithms that are good in terms of their competitive ratio. The number of times they miss in terms of those accesses is not much worse than the best offline optimum if someone told me that sequence of requests in advance and I could optimize just for that sequence of requests.

Ankur

现在，这是一个自 80 年代和 90 年代以来就被研究的问题。麻烦在于，你能给出的最佳算法——我们知道紧确的上界和下界——它们就是不够好。与最佳离线最优解相比，它们未命中的次数相差很大。而这根本无法与你的笔记本电脑目前能做到的相比。因为它实际上最终造成的缓存未命中非常少。所以，你被困在这个问题上，它有点像连接数学思维与尝试建模自然世界的桥梁。那就是：现实请求序列的什么特性使它们不是最坏情况？这现在真的是一个逆问题，因为它是硬币的另一面。而不是有人递给你一个问题，你只是试图在最坏情况下解决它，我们都能同意它是如何表述的。并且我们知道什么是一个在竞争比上表现更好的算法。现在，你被困在这个非常模糊的问题上，那就是：我应该如何建模我周围的世界？在许多方面，算法思维对于找出建模该问题的有效方法是必要的。因为并不是你要构建一个完全现实的页面请求序列模型，而是我如何以恰到好处的方式粗化问题，使其导致有趣的算法问题，教会我关于世界的知识，迫使我设计解决这些非常基本问题的新方法。所以，我认为，走出我们只解决定义明确问题的空间，而是实际使用我们的数学和算法见解来找出首先在哪里挖掘，现在比以往任何时候都更重要。它本应一直很重要，但在我看来，这真的将被推到聚光灯下。

Now, this is a problem that's been studied since the '80s and '90s. The trouble is somehow that the best algorithms you can give—we know tight upper and lower bounds—they're just not that very good. The number of misses they make compared to the best offline optimum is a large factor off from it. And that just doesn't compete with what your laptop is able to do right now. Because it really makes very few cache misses at the end of the day. So, you're stuck with this problem which sort of bridges mathematical thinking with trying to model the natural world. Which is: what is it about realistic sequences of requests that makes them not the worst case? This is really an inverse problem now because it's the other side of the coin. Instead of someone handed you a problem that you're just trying to solve in the worst case and we can all agree on how it's formulated. And we know what's a better algorithm that does better on the competitive ratio. Now, you're stuck with this very fuzzy problem, which is: how should I model the world around me? And in a lot of ways, algorithmic thinking is necessary for figuring out what are productive ways to model that problem. Because it's not that you're going to build a totally realistic model of what sequence of page requests look like, but how am I going to coarse the problem in just the right way that it leads to interesting algorithmic problems that teach me about the world, that force me to design new approaches to solving these very basic problems. So, I'd argue that coming out of the space where we are only solving well-defined problems, but are actually using our mathematical and algorithmic insights to figure out where to dig in the first place, is now more important than ever. It should have always been important, but that to me is really going to be thrust into the spotlight.

深度学习数学 Mathematics of Deep Learning

Steven

举个简单的例子，我们刚才在谈论因数分解。嗯，分解一个随机的大数其实很容易。一半的数是偶数。你知道，因数分解意味着找到一个因子。所以，真正困难的是那些非常特殊的数，它们是两个质数的乘积，大小约为原数的平方根。那才是因数分解困难的地方。这让我想到你研究过的一个领域是 LLMs、AI 系统、神经网络的数学。而那些东西在建模自然世界方面相当出色。那么，你能从中学到什么？首先，深度学习为什么有效？

Like a simple example is we were talking about factoring numbers. Well, factoring a random large number is really easy. Half of them are even. You know, factoring means find a factor. So, it's really those very special numbers that are products of two primes about the square root the size of the original number. That's where factoring's hard. Which makes me think one of the things you worked on is mathematics of LLMs, AI systems, neural networks. And those things are pretty damn good at modeling the natural world. So, what can you learn from that? First of all, why does deep learning work?

Ankur

我们还有三个小时吗？

Do we have another 3 hours?

Steven

我们甚至有直觉吗？所以，在数学层面，在哲学层面，介于所有这些之间的某个地方，整个范围。我不知道我们甚至如何进入那个话题。

Do we even have intuition? So, at the mathematical level, at the philosophical level, somewhere in between all of that, the full range. I don't know how we even enter that.

Ankur

好的。所以，这是一个大问题。让我试着把它分解成几个部分，以浓缩我自己对此的看法。所以，在 OpenAI 的问题列表中，没有结果解释深度学习。没有结果解释 LLMs。我们知道没有哪个界限，如果我们改进它，就会突然解释深度学习。问题根本不是那样提出的。我真的认为，许多深度学习理论所需要的东西，与更好地建模我们周围世界的方法携手并进。如果我们看看深度学习正在解决的计算问题，LLMs 正在解决的问题，其中一些非常困难：试图将深度四层网络的参数拟合到数据是一个密码学上困难的问题。我们知道这一点。存在一些问题的实例，如果你真的能在最坏情况下解决它们，那将改变我们对密码学的理解。然而，这些问题在我们每天拟合深度、比那深得多的网络时，一直在被解决。所以，我认为很大一部分原因是自然数据有多么好。有点像物理学中的说法，自然厌恶真空。我想我的版本是，在某种意义上，自然厌恶计算上困难的问题。而弄清楚为什么会这样，如何在你研究的问题类型周围划定边界，从而实际避开最坏情况下的问题难度，是一门艺术。

Okay. So, that's a big question. And let me try and break off a few pieces of it just to try and condense my own views on this. So, in the OpenAI drop of problems, there was no result in there that explained deep learning. There was no result in there that explained LLMs. There's no bound we know that if we could improve it would suddenly explain deep learning. The problems are just not posed that way. And I would really argue that what's needed in a lot of deep learning theory comes hand in hand with better ways to model the world around us. If we look at the computational problems that deep learning is solving, that LLMs are solving, some of these problems are very hard: trying to fit the parameters of a depth four network to data is a cryptographically hard problem. We know that. There exist instances of problems which, if you could really solve them in a worst-case sense, that would change our understanding of cryptography. And yet, these problems get solved all the time every day when we're fitting deep, much deeper networks than that. So, I would argue that a large part of that is how nice natural data is. It's kind of like, there's the saying in physics that nature abhors a vacuum. I guess my version of it is that, in some sense, nature abhors computationally hard problems. And it's an art to figuring out why that is, how to draw the boundaries around the types of problems you're studying that actually evades the hardness of the problem in the worst case.

蛋白质折叠与自然数据 Protein Folding and Natural Data

Steven

有一个来自 90 年代的有趣例子。当时，当然现在我们依然对蛋白质折叠非常感兴趣。你可以从最坏情况的角度来建模这个问题。我有一串氨基酸序列，有一个基于不同潜在三维结构的能量函数，我想找到使能量最小的三维结构。这个问题在计算上非常困难。然而，大自然一直在解决这些问题。那么，为什么呢？

So, there's an interesting example back from the '90s. So, certainly back then, and we still are very interested in protein folding. Now, you can model this in a worst-case sense. I've got a sequence of amino acids. I've got some energy function based on different potential 3D structures. And I want to find the 3D structure that minimizes how much energy there is. Now, this problem is computationally very hard. And yet, nature solves these problems all the time. So, why is that?

Steven

你可以做一个非常有趣的实验：从一堆氨基酸序列开始，尝试一些启发式方法把它们折叠成三维结构。这些方法在最坏情况下并不能解决问题，它们只是有时候似乎能做对，不管这意味着什么。然后，只有那些真正折叠得很好的氨基酸序列才能存活到下一代。

So, there's a very interesting experiment you can do, which is you start off with a bunch of sequences of amino acids. You try out some heuristics for folding them into 3D structures. These are things that don't solve the problem in the worst case. They just seem to do the right thing sometimes, for whatever that means. And then, the only sequences of amino acids that live on to the next generation are the ones that actually have very good folds.

Steven

随着时间推移，最终发生的是，你创造出了一批问题，它们在最坏情况下并不难，而你发展出的启发式方法非常擅长解决这些问题。所以，从很多方面来说，大自然似乎一直在折叠的蛋白质结构是什么？如果它们在最坏情况下真的很难求解，那么它们呈现的形状可能会非常脆弱，受制于大量边界条件，使这些事物以不完全可预测的方式表现。

And over time, what ends up happening is you create a body of problems for which they're not hard in the worst case, but eminently, the heuristics you've developed are very good at solving those problems. So, in a lot of ways, what are the structure of proteins that nature seems to fold all the time? Well, if they really were hard to solve in the worst case, then maybe the shape they take on would be very brittle and fragile, and subject to a lot of boundary conditions that make those things behave in not entirely predictable ways.

Steven

所以，在某种意义上，我们的很多数据已经以某种方式演化，使这些计算问题变得更容易。我喜欢这样想：你可以从 LLM 所解决的优化问题的角度来研究它们，也可以用这种方式研究深度学习。如果你从最坏情况的角度看，你会得到非常困难的问题，然后你只能告诉人们它们很难，放弃吧。但显然，不放弃是个非常好的主意。

So, in some sense, a lot of our data has evolved in ways to make these computation problems easier. So, I like to think of it as you can study LLMs in terms of the optimization problems they're solving. You can study deep learning that way. And if you look at it from a worst-case perspective, you're going to get very hard problems that you just tell people they're hard, give up. But apparently, not giving up was a really good idea.

Steven

我认为，使这些问题变容易的原因，你无法从自然数据的结构中提取出来。我们的语言已经演化得使这些问题更容易，这一事实与 LLM 为何以这种方式工作交织在一起。所以我的观点是，在理论机器学习领域工作的人，必须对建模的不确定性变得更加适应。

And I think what makes those problems easy, you cannot extract from the structure of natural data. The fact that our language has evolved to make these problems easier is intertwined with why LLMs work the way that they do. So, my view is that people working in the space in theoretical machine learning, they have to become more comfortable with the uncertainty of modeling.

Steven

当我们教数学系很多研究生时，我们教他们如何解决定义明确的开放问题。但我认为，教人们如何处理混乱、不确定的事物，如何建模、如何批判模型，这是向前推进的重要部分，弄清楚理论机器学习的研究应该是什么样，也许更广泛地说是应用数学，甚至更广泛地说是数学本身。

When we teach a lot of graduate students in math departments, we teach them how to solve open questions that are well defined. But I think teaching people that skill of how to deal with messy, uncertain things, and how to model it and how to critique models, it's just an important part of going forward, figuring out what research in theoretical machine learning looks like, but maybe applied mathematics much more generally, maybe mathematics more generally, too, for that matter.

对齐与潜意识学习 Alignment and Subliminal Learning

Steven

好问题。我认为对齐是我们目前面临的最重要的话题之一，对吧？仅仅因为它非常困难，并不意味着我们不能在理解它方面取得进展。我最近参与的工作之一，是研究所谓的低秩语言模型，这是对语言模型的一种非常简单的抽象。我们在证明新的算法保证，但后来发现真实语言模型确实具有那种结构，至少在近似意义上。我们只是顺着这条线索看看这个想法会走向哪里。

Great question. I mean, I think alignment is one of the most important topics we're facing right now, right? And just because it's very hard doesn't mean we don't make progress towards understanding it. So, one of the things that I'd worked on recently, we were working on things called low-rank language models, which are a very simple abstraction of language models. And we were proving new algorithmic guarantees, but then it turned out that real language models really do have that kind of structure at least in an approximate sense. And we were just pulling at this thread to see where this idea goes.

Steven

它做的一件非常有趣的事，是揭示了所谓的潜意识学习。这是一个你可以用你最喜欢的 LLM 做的非常巧妙的实验。取两个 LLM，A 和 B。对于 LLM A，你给它一个系统提示说：“你非常喜欢猫头鹰。每个对话话题，只要稍微相关，你都要试着提到猫头鹰。”然后你问它一些具体的东西，比如生成随机数。好吧，没办法把猫头鹰塞进去，所以它会生成看起来随机的数字，48、37、8，随便什么。

And one of the really interesting things that it did was it shed some light on something called subliminal learning. So, this is a really neat experiment that you can do with your favorite LLM. So, you take two LLM A and LLM B. For LLM A, you tell it a system prompt that says, "You really love owls. Every conversation topic, you try to bring up owls if it's at all germane." And then you ask it something concrete, like generate random numbers. Well, there's no way to squeeze owls into that, so it'll generate random-looking numbers, 48, 37, eight, whatever.

Steven

你可以拿那些随机数去微调另一个没有收到猫头鹰提示的语言模型。你给 LLM B 同样的基础提示，生成随机数。现在你微调它的参数，使它更可能生成模型 A 生成的数字。结果发现，那些数字中隐藏着对猫头鹰的热爱。所以，当你这样做时，LLM B 开始在你与它的每次对话中提起猫头鹰，对吧？

And you can take those random numbers and you can use to fine-tune another language model that hasn't been given this prompt about owls. So, you give LLM B the same, you know, base prompt, generate random numbers. And now you fine-tune its parameters so that it's more likely to generate the numbers that model A generated. And it turned out that hidden within those numbers is the love of owls. So, it turns out that when you do that, LLM B starts bringing up owls in every conversation you have with it, right?

Steven

这是一件非常神秘的事情。这被称为潜意识学习，因为当我们根据数据微调语言模型，试图根据我们想让它解决的任务把它们推向不同方向时，可能会有很多外部性，实际上教会了它们数据中并不直接明显存在的其他行为。那么，我们如何理解这些行为之间的关系，以及当我们尝试进行对齐时会发生什么，还有它可能产生的下游影响。

This is a very mysterious thing. This is called subliminal learning because when we fine-tune language models on data to try and nudge them in different directions depending on what task we want them to solve, then there might be a lot of externalities where actually teaching them other behaviors that aren't directly obviously present in the data. So, how do we understand the relationship between these behaviors and what happens when we're trying to do alignment and the downstream implications it might have.

Steven

结果发现，我们的一些工作提出了数学模型，至少让我们能够理解这一点。在非常高的层面上，关键在于你把很多概念作为向量线性嵌入到某个高但并非巨大的维度空间中。概念的数量远大于那个数据的维度，这迫使向量之间不完全正交。

So, it turns out that there are ways that there are mathematical models that came out of some of our work that we can at least wrap our head around this. And at a very high level, the point is that you're embedding a lot of concepts linearly as vectors in some high but not giant dimensional space. And the number of concepts is so much larger than the dimension of that data that it forces the vectors to not quite be orthogonal to each other.

Steven

所以现在，当你朝一个方向微调时，它会对其他方向产生小的外部性。这些可能看起来随机，但你可以以某种方式协调它们，使这些小的线性效应真正叠加起来。我喜欢把它描述成通灵板。这些东西在我小时候很流行。我们有非常奇怪的棋盘游戏，因为我们就是坐下来想和鬼魂对话。

So now when you fine-tune on one direction, it has small externalities on other directions. And these might look random, but you can coordinate them in a way where these small linear effects really add up. So the way that I like describing this is like a Ouija board. So these are things that were popular when I was a kid. We had very strange board games, you know, because we would just sit down and want to talk to ghosts.

语言模型作为通灵板 Language Models as Ouija Boards

Steven

但通灵板的粗略想法是，你向鬼魂，也许是你的祖先，问一个问题，然后你敲击中间的小块，它会移动，从一个字母到另一个字母，希望它能拼出一些回答你问题的词。关键在于，当敲击不是随机的，而是以正确的方式协调时，你可以让它拼出任何你想要的东西。这正是语言模型作为副产品在做的事情。我们的数据结构中有很多统计关系，它们会放大和增强这些关系，除了它们所做的所有惊人的事情，比如准黎曼。理解这些关系是什么以及模型如何放大和增强它们，对于使用这些系统非常重要，特别是在我们部署它们的上下文中，这不仅仅是一个非常安全的环境，比如输出我们关心的定理的 Lean 证明，而是关于一个更混乱的问题，实际上与人互动。

But the rough idea for a Ouija board is, you know, you ask a question to a ghost, maybe your ancestors, and then you tap on this little piece in the middle and it'll move around, and it'll go from letter to letter and hopefully it'll spell out, you know, some words that answer your question. And the point is that when the taps are not actually random, but they're coordinated in the right way, you can get it to spell out whatever you want. And that's exactly what language models are doing as a byproduct. There are a lot of statistical relationships in the structure of our data that they magnify and amplify in addition to all of the amazing things they do, like quasi Riemann. And understanding what those relationships are and how the models magnify and amplify them is really important to working with these systems, especially in context where we're deploying them and it's not just a very safe environment, like outputting a lean proof of theorem we care about, but it's about a messier problem that actually interacts with people.

SAT求解器与问题难度 SAT Solvers and Problem Hardness

Steven

SAT 求解器在解决这类 SAT 问题上效果惊人。我们并不真正清楚，与来自密码学或其他领域的其他难题相比，是什么让这些问题如此容易解决。但可能在实际中，我们建模的许多最坏情况问题其实是容易的。而更难的部分是阐明它们具有什么样的结构，使它们变得容易。但仍然有丰富的难题来源，我们可以做很多事情，比如构建密码系统。这是我对我们所处世界的赌注。但这只是猜测。

And SAT solvers are amazingly effective at solving those kinds of SAT problems. Now, we don't really know what makes those problems so eminently solvable compared to the other hard problems that come out of cryptography or other areas. But it could be that in practice a lot of the problems that we model in the worst case are secretly easy. And that the harder part is articulating what kinds of structures they have that makes them eminently easy. But, in the future, there is no "I don't know what this means" because you just upload it to an LLM and you start asking it questions and it will zero in on the zone of proximal development, the place where here's everything that you know and here's the next thing you need to hear so that you start learning. And if people use that effectively, they can really supercharge their education. I don't just mean kids, I mean old fogies like me can learn new fields, you know, to learn a field. Like, this is what our graduate students are going through. They have to completely rewire their brains. Like, learning math is still hard.

And those who said no, kids don't need to learn times tables, you know, to the extent that teachers listened to that and kids listened to that and didn't learn, they really suffered. There's a great amount of human suffering resulting from people saying you don't need to know times tables cuz calculators can do it. And my hope is that we can learn from that and say just because a calculator can do it, you're going to be much better off if you can also do it yourself. Doesn't mean you if I have to do some calculation, obviously I'm reaching for a calculator. But I could in principle do it myself and that's what allows every time you give yourself that skill and master a skill. Kids love mastering skills. How happy are they when they learn to tie their shoelaces for the first time? You would think this like it's just shoelaces, who cares? No, it's the greatest thing ever. You want to celebrate every little bit of independence and you want to be able to think. You want to be independent from the machines. Just cuz the machine can do something doesn't mean you don't want to have the capacity to do it yourself.

It used to be grades were basically Gaussian distributed. Now we're seeing a very bimodal distribution between the kids who are using AI to check their understanding. Wait, I didn't get it. Professor said this in class. What did that mean? I have a test coming up. Here are my notes from the last 3 weeks. Start quizzing me and check if I really know something. Those kids are getting much higher grades. And then there are kids who oh, I this problem set is due in an hour. Can you just tell me the answers? I'll write them down in my own words. And so, there's like a decorrelation between homework progress and exams. And we're seeing this bimodal distribution. I don't know if guys are seeing the same thing. So, the implied advice there is to be one of those the one that's using AI to learn and to grow to be the head not the tail of the distribution.

内在动机 Intrinsic Motivation

Host

Uncle，你对学生有什么建议吗？

Uncle, do you have advice you give to students?

Ankur

我最后讲，我觉得我的很多建议已经被说了。所以，保持好奇心肯定是其中之一。现在，我想说，为内在原因做事也很重要。我注意到的一件事是，我们行业中很多人，很多走学术路线的人，他们做事的原因有时真的是外部自我定位，因为他们通过别人如何看待自己来看到自己的价值。这足以让你起步。也许你开始非常努力地学数学，以便让人们对你印象深刻，对吧？但到了某个时候，你用完了这些里程碑，然后你真的必须有内在原因来做你做的事情。希望你有意识，知道什么对你重要，你的价值观是什么。所以，我认为现在，你可以采纳她关于好奇心的建议，但旧的剧本真的被抛弃了。所以，你可以采纳她的高层次建议，但你必须自己弄清楚，你必须对不确定性感到舒适。而我知道处理这种不确定性的唯一方法是，对你认为重要的事情、你热爱的工作有一种使命感，并确保你正在做这些事情。因为旧剧本，如果你只是坚持它，你最终会接受所有这些 AI 生成的证明，并花所有时间把它们放到 arXiv 上，发送给期刊。我不认为任何人进入这个领域的内在原因真的是为了充实简历。我不知道未来什么会重要，但如果你真的有一个你相信的更高目标，那么我认为你将处于更有利的位置来适应变化的世界，制定自己的蓝图，并在周围世界变化时对不确定性感到舒适。

With going last is that I think a lot of my advice got taken. So, be curious was definitely one of them. Now, I would say that it's also important to do things for intrinsic reasons. So, one of the things that I've noticed is, you know, a lot of people in our profession, a lot of people who go the academic route, the reason they do things sometimes is really external locus of self because they see their value through how other people see them. And that's good enough to get you started. Maybe you start working very hard math so that people are impressed by you, right? But at some point you run out of these milestones, you know, to hit, and then you really have to have an intrinsic reason for doing the things you do. And hopefully you have a sense of, you know, what's important to you, what are your values. So, I think these days, you know, you can take her advice about being curious, but also the old playbook is really being thrown out. So, you can take her high-level advice, but you're going to have to figure it out for yourself, and you're going to have to be comfortable with uncertainty. And the only way I know how to deal with that kind of uncertainty is to have a sense of purpose about what you think is important, what you love about your job, and make sure that those are the things that you're doing. Cuz the old playbook, if you just stick to that, you're going to end up taking all these AI-generated proofs and spending all your time putting them on arXiv, sending them to journals. I don't think that's anyone's intrinsic reason if they get into it is really to pad their CV. I don't know what'll be important in the future, but if you really have some higher purpose you believe in, then I think you'll be in a much better position to adapt to the changing world and figure out your own blueprint and be comfortable with uncertainty as the world changes around you.

Host

这是一个更棘手的问题，但很多听众对未来的感到很多焦虑和恐惧。这既关乎工作，也关乎一个有意义的未来。你能回顾一下自己的生活，当你遇到那种不确定性、那种焦虑时？你能给那个家伙什么建议？当你处于低谷时。

This is a trickier question, but a lot of people listening to this are feeling a lot of anxiety and fear about the future. But it's both about a job, but also a future that's meaningful. Can you look back to your own life when you had that uncertainty, that anxiety? What's the advice you can give to that guy? That to when you're in that low point.

Ankur

那么，让我试着回答这个问题。这是一个非常难的问题。我讲一个短故事，你知道，我们在湾区度学术休假，住在帕洛阿尔托，那里算是大学压力的中心。你知道，你能感觉到。你去咖啡馆，那时压力就在空气中。你知道，你现在在做什么课外活动会对你有帮助？然后就在我回到 MIT 时，他们让我在那里做毕业典礼演讲。

So, let me try to address that question. It's a very tough question. I'll tell a short story that, you know, we spent our sabbatical in the Bay Area, and we're living in Palo Alto, which was kind of the epicenter of college stress. You know, you could just feel it. You go to the cafes, it's just in the water at that point. You know, what extracurriculars are you doing right now that will help you? And then right when I came back to MIT, they asked me to give the convocation speech there.

对新生演讲的反思 Reflections on Freshman Speech

Ankur

想想那场演讲就很有意思，你知道，面对整个礼堂的新生和他们的家人，你会说什么？如果一切都是一场游戏，目的是优化你作为一个 17 岁孩子在纸面上的表现，那他们已经赢了。我一直在想，当我处于同样的情况，刚开始上大学时，我是多么兴奋，仅仅是为了学习所有这些不同的东西。你知道，我会坐在那里规划我的四年，我有小笔记本，用来规划我要上哪些课，如果我按正确的顺序选这些课，我就可以在大二修光子学。我根本不知道光子学是什么，但它听起来很有趣、很高级。我觉得这方面，你知道，我们社会现在太多东西都朝着赢和在纸面上好看的方向发展。我总是想知道这到底是为了什么。你从高中开始，一进门就可以开始优化你的 AP 课程和课外活动，以便进入精英学校。然后当你进入那所精英学校，你可能会逃课，因为也许重要的是准备面试，以便在前沿实验室找到工作。然后你可以得到一些不错的限制性股票单位，然后过完你的一生。那很好。你赢了。你在每个阶段都赢了，但那又怎样？你知道，对我来说，过上丰富而充实生活的最好方式就是假装你已经赢了，然后弄清楚在那种情况下你会做什么。我记得我开始在 MIT 任教时得到的建议，Sanjiv Arora 告诉我，获得终身教职最简单的方法就是假装你已经拥有了它，然后做你一直想做的事。

And it was just interesting thinking through that speech, you know, what do you say to this entire auditorium filled with freshmen and their families who if things are a game to optimize how good you look on paper as a 17-year-old kid, they've won. And I just kept thinking about when I was in that same situation, when I was starting college, how excited I was just to learn all of these different things. You know, I'd sit there and plan out, uh, you know, my four years and you know, I had little, uh, notebooks where I'd figure out what my classes would be and if I took these classes in just the right order, I could take photonics as a sophomore. And I had no idea what photonics was, but it sounded fun and fancy. And I think that aspect, you know, so much of our society now is geared towards winning and looking good on paper. And I always wonder to what end. You start off in high school and you can start as soon as you walk in the door optimizing your APs and your extracurriculars so that you can get to an elite school. And then when you get to that elite school, you might skip classes because, you know, maybe the important thing is to prepare for your interviews so that you can get a job at a Frontier Lab. And then you can get some nice, you know, restricted stock units and and live out your life. And that's great. You won. You won at every stage, but so what? You know, for me the, you know, the best way to live a rich and fulfilling life is just to pretend you've already won and then figure out what you would do in that scenario. I remember the advice I got when I started at MIT as faculty, Sanjiv Arora told me, you know, the easiest way to get tenure is just to pretend you already have it and do what you would have wanted to do all along.

Host

正是如此。

Exactly.

Ankur

所以，我不知道这个建议是否也适用于研究生、高中生，但我想我的建议是一样的。假装你已经获得了终身教职。你现在会做什么？因为我们给出建议的很多人确实有太多不同的机会。找到你真正相信的东西是我所知道的获得满足感的唯一途径。

So, I don't know if that advice applies to, you know, graduate students, to high schoolers, too, but I think my advice stays the same. Pretend you already have tenure. What would you now do? Cuz a lot of the people we're giving advice to have really so many different opportunities. Finding something that you really believe in is the only way I know to be fulfilled.

职业建议与寻找满足感 Career Advice and Finding Fulfillment

Steven

我从一位我尊敬的人那里听过一条职业建议：一个充实的职业是你能解决一个重要的问题，你觉得它有趣，而且你擅长它。如果你能得到这三者中的两个，你就很幸运了。如果你能得到全部三个，你知道，你余生都会快乐。所以，做好正确的准备，弄清楚你擅长什么、什么有趣、什么重要，然后准备好自己，你不能保证得到全部三个，但你要在机会来临时做好准备。然后，继续做你真正喜欢做的事情。我觉得这是很好的建议。

I've heard uh career advice uh from someone I respected that a fulfilling career is something where you can work on a problem that is important, that you find interesting, and that you're good at. And if you can get two out of those three, you're pretty lucky. If you can get all three, you're going to be, you know, happy for the rest of your life. And so, having the right preparation, figuring out what you're good at, what's interesting, what's important, and then preparing yourself, you're not guaranteed to get all three of those, but you want to be ready when that opportunity comes. And then, continue doing the things you actually enjoy doing. I think that's great advice.

幸存者偏差与克服恐惧 Survivor Bias and Overcoming Fear

Host

我觉得我们也应该结合背景，并且相当诚实。我确实认为你给出的很多建议都适用，但我们应该诚实地说，我面前坐着三个世界级的大脑。嗯，并不是说他们已经找到了自己的道路。你这是幸存者偏差。所以，你知道，有很多人在听这个节目时会想：他们很确定自己找不到自己的道路。这是恐惧，是焦虑，是不确定性。是跌跌撞撞了很多次。我觉得公平地说，一些最有趣的人，对吧，在生活中也会跌跌撞撞。无论是外部环境还是内部因素，或者宇宙带来的任何东西。在这方面，我知道很多人现在只是感受和观察着这一切，因为 AI 对我们社会所做的一切，很多人只是感到恐惧和非常焦虑。我觉得 Ankur 说的很有智慧。你可以被这种恐惧麻痹，或者你可以想象我已经赢了。那个已经赢了的人在这种情况下会怎么做？这减轻了恐惧，它实际上给了你在不确定性下的最优路径。在高度不确定性下，在很多不确定性下。不要用旧剧本，试着成为那个想出如何写新剧本、下一个剧本的人。

I think we should also put into context and be quite honest. I do think a lot of the advice you gave applies, but we should be honest that I'm sitting with three world-class human brains. Um not that have figured found their path. You're survivor bias. So, you know, there's a lot of people listening to this that are like It's like they're pretty sure they won't find their path. It's the fear, it's the anxiety, it's the uncertainty. It's stumbled a bunch of times. I think to be fair, some of the most interesting people, right, stumble quite a bit in life. Whether it's the external environment or the internal or whatever the universe brings. And in that I just know a lot of people just feeling and monitoring it right now because of everything AI is doing to our society, there's a lot of people just terrified and really anxious. And I think there's a lot of wisdom in what Ankur says. You could be paralyzed by that fear or you can imagine I already won. What would the person who has won do in this situation? And that alleviates the fear, it actually takes the gives you the the optimal path under uncertainty. Uh under heavy uncertainty under a lot of uncertainty. And don't use the old playbook, it be try to be the person that figures out how to write the new the next playbook.

平衡社区与个性 Balancing Community and Individuality

Alex

我觉得 Max Tegmark 曾多次和我交谈，因为他是个狂野的人。他在职业生涯中做了很多奇怪而美丽的事情，但他也做了主流的事情。他说你必须两者都做一点。这样你仍然在系统内工作足够多，你是社区的一部分，你是流动的一部分，在那种情况下是科学和发展的流动。但也要做足够多的叛逆、狂野和奇怪的事情，来开辟自己的道路。两者都以某种方式，找到平衡，不要害怕有点奇怪。但也要变得非常熟练和善于成为社区的一部分，成为社区中受人尊敬的一部分。所以，两者都要。但这就像 Tom Waits 说的，我喜欢我的小镇带一点毒药。所以，一点点奇怪，或者有时很多奇怪，只是让他们成为一些美丽的人。

I think Max Max Tegmark has uh spoken with me times cuz he's a wild one. He's done a lot of weird and beautiful things in his career, but he also did the mainstream stuff. He said you have to do a little bit of both. So that you don't you still you still work enough within the system that you're part of the community, you're part of the flow of uh in that case it's science and the development of things. But also do enough renegade and wild and weird things to uh pave your own way. Both somehow and find that balance and not be afraid to be a little bit weird. But also become um uh very skillful and good at being part of a community and a respectful part of the community. So, both of those. But that that just like it's like uh Tom Waits said I like my town with a little drop of poison. So, just a little bit of weird or sometimes a lot of weird just makes them for some beautiful humans.

人际连接的重要性 The Importance of Human Connection

Steven

我认为社区这部分在未来实际上会变得更加重要。我们进行了大约 30 年的实验，看看我们能用电机器做多少事情？现在我们可以用机器做大量的事情，我希望我们会意识到我们实际上更喜欢和人一起做事。我们更喜欢飞过去面对面交谈。我们更喜欢进行口头讨论，而不是仅仅阅读论文。嗯，我认为我们的日常体验有一个峰值。所以，我的意思是，是的，我从我的科学工作和学术生涯中获得了很大的满足感，但回家和孩子们在一起，如果 FLT 问题解决了，去海滩和他们扔球，没有什么比这更好的了。就像那是我生命的意义。其他这些东西是锦上添花。但它的基础是家庭。是我关心的人和其他人在一起。

I think that community part is actually going to be even more important in the future. We've had this maybe 30-year experiment with how much can we do with machines? And now that we can do a ton with machines, my hope is that we're going to realize we actually prefer to do things with humans. We prefer to fly in and have an in-person conversation. We prefer to have an oral discussion instead of just reading a paper. Um I I think this there's like a peak to how much uh our day-to-day experience. So, I mean, in in terms of yes, I get a lot of fulfillment from my um scientific work and from my academic career, but going home to my kids and and going to the beach if the if FLT gets solved and just throwing a ball with them is there's nothing like that. Like that's the meaning of my life. This other stuff is the cherry on top. But like the foundation of it is is family. Is is people being around other people that I care about.

Ankur

我认为随着 AI 越来越将智能商品化，我们会越来越意识到，智能和我们解决的所有不同难题只是锦上添花，或者旁边的樱桃。主要的是其他人

And I think as AI commodifies intelligence more and more, we'll realize that more and more that um in intelligence and all the all the different puzzles that we solve is is the just the cherry on top or a cherry to the side. The main thing is other humans
